The polytope algebra of generalized permutahedra
Jose Bastidas

Abstract
. The polytope subalgebra of deformations of a zonotope can be endowed with the structure of a module over the Tits algebra of the corresponding hyperplane arrangement. We explore this construction and find relations between statistics on (signed) permutations and the module structure in the case of (type B) generalized permutahedra. In type B, the module structure surprisingly reveals that any family of generators (via signed Minkowski sums) for generalized permutahedra of type B will contain at least 2 d − 1 2^{d-1} full-dimensional polytopes. We find a generating family of simplices attaining this minimum. Finally, we prove that the relations defining the polytope algebra are compatible with the Hopf monoid structure of generalized permutahedra.



1.
Introduction

Generalized permutahedra serve as a geometric model for many
classical
(type A) combinatorial objects and have been extensively studied in resent years. Notably, Aguiar and Ardila
[
1
]
endowed generalized permutahedra with the structure of a Hopf monoid
GP
in the category of species and, in so doing, gave a unified framework to study similar algebraic structures over many different families of combinatorial objects. At the same time, McMullen’s polytope algebra
[
21
]
offers a different algebraic perspective to study generalized permutahedra.

One of the goals of the present paper is to investigate the compatibility between both structures.
To achieve this goal, we take a more general approach and study deformations of an arbitrary zonotope.
We then specialize our results to deformations Coxeter permutahedra of type A and type B, revealing remarkable connections with (type B) Eulerian polynomials and statistics over (signed) permutations. The results in type B allow us to find a family of generalized permutahedra that generates all other deformations via signed Minkowski sums, thus solving a question posed by Ardila, Castillo, Eur, and Postnikov
[
7
]
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional real vector space.
The polytope algebra
\Pi(V)
is generated by the classes
[{\mathfrak{p}}]
of polytopes
{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq V
.
These classes satisfy the following
valuation
and
translation invariance
relations:

[{\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}]+[{\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}]=[{\mathfrak{p}}]+[{\mathfrak{q}}]
and
[{\mathfrak{p}}+\{t\}]=[{\mathfrak{p}}],

whenever
{\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}
is a polytope and for every
t\in V
.
The product structure of
\Pi(V)
is given by the Minkowski sum of polytopes:
[{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot[{\mathfrak{q}}]=[{\mathfrak{p}}+{\mathfrak{q}}]
.

Figure 1.1.
Different expressions for the class of the trapezoid above in the polytope algebra
\Pi(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

For a fixed polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq V
,
\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})
denotes the subalgebra of
\Pi(V)
generated by classes of
deformations
of
{\mathfrak{p}}
; see
[
22
]
.
We are particularly interested in the case where
{\mathfrak{p}}
is a zonotope corresponding to a linear hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{A}
.
In this case, let
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
denote the Tits algebra of
\mathcal{A}
, see Section
3
.
It is linearly generated by the elements
\mathtt{H}_{F}
as
F
runs through the faces of the arrangement.
The following is a central result of this paper.

Theorem
4.3
.

Let
{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\mathcal{A}
be as above.
The algebra
\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})
is a right
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
-module under the following action:

[{\mathfrak{q}}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}:=[{\mathfrak{q}}_{v}],

where
v\in\relint(F)
and
{\mathfrak{q}}_{v}
denotes the face of
{\mathfrak{q}}

maximal in the direction
of
v
.
Moreover, the action of
\mathtt{H}_{F}
is an endomorphism of graded algebras.

In the particular case of the permutahedron and the braid arrangement, the compatibility between the algebra structure and the action of the Tits algebra is related to the Hopf monoid structure of Aguiar and Ardila.
Similar results for the Hopf monoid of extended generalized permutahedra were independently obtained by Ardila and Sanchez in
[
8
]
.

Theorem
7.3
.

The species
\Pi
defined by
\Pi[I]=\Pi(\pi_{I})
, where
\pi_{I}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{I}
is the standard permutahedron, is a Hopf monoid quotient of
{\sf GP}
.

We embark on further understanding the module structure of
\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})
when
{\mathfrak{z}}
is a zonotope corresponding to a hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{A}
.
The decomposition of
\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})=\bigoplus_{r}\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}})
into its graded components will play an essential role here. McMullen characterized
\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}})
as the eigenspace of the dilation morphism
\delta_{\lambda}
, defined by
\delta_{\lambda}[{\mathfrak{p}}]=[\lambda{\mathfrak{p}}]
, with eigenvalue
\lambda^{r}
for any positive
\lambda\neq 1
.
Theorem
4.3
then implies that each graded component is a
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
-submodule.

The simple modules over
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
are one-dimensional and indexed by the flats of the arrangement
[
5
, Chapter 9]
.
Given a module
M
over
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
, the number of copies of the simple module associated with the flat
\mathrm{X}
that appear as a composition factor of
M
is
\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(M)
.
We propose that studying these algebraic invariants for the modules
\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}})
yields important geometric and combinatorial information of the deformations of
{\mathfrak{z}}
.
In Sections
5
and
6
, we do this for the algebra of deformations of the permutahedron and the type B permutahedron, respectively.
The main results in these sections relate the invariants
\eta_{\mathrm{X}}
with statistics over (signed) permutations:

Theorem
5.1
.

For any flat
\mathrm{X}
of the braid arrangement in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
r=0,1,\dots,d-1
,

\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}(\pi_{d}))=\big|\{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}\,:\,\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X},\ \exc(\sigma)=r\}\big|.

Theorem
6.1
.

For any flat
\mathrm{X}
of type B Coxeter arrangement in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
r=0,1,\dots,d
,

\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}(\pi^{B}_{d}))=\big|\{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}\,:\,\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X},\ \exc_{B}(\sigma)=r\}\big|.

This surprising relation arises from the remarkable results of McMullen and Brenti that we explain now. McMullen
[
22
]
proved that when
{\mathfrak{p}}
is a simple polytope, just like the (type B) permutahedron, the dimension of the graded components
\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{p}})
are determined by the
h
-numbers of
{\mathfrak{p}}
. On the other hand, building on top of work by Björner
[
10
]
, Brenti proved that the
h
-polynomial of the Coxeter permutahedron associated with any Coxeter group is the corresponding Eulerian polynomial
[
12
, Theorem 2.3]
.

Work of Postnikov
[
26
]
, and of Ardila, Benedetti, and Doker
[
6
]
show that any generalized permutahedron in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be written as the
signed Minkowski sum
of the faces of the standard simplex
\Delta_{[d]}=\Conv\{e_{j}\,:\,j\in[d]\}
. As a consequence of Theorem
6.1
, we show that any such set of generators for type B generalized permutahedra contains at least
2^{d-1}
full dimensional polytopes (see Proposition
6.8
). We manage to obtain a family of generators that attains this minimum.

Theorem
6.9
.

Every type B generalized permutahedron in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be written uniquely as a signed Minkowski sum of the simplices
\Delta_{S}
and
\Delta^{0}_{S}
for
special involution-exclusive
subsets
S\subseteq[\pm d]
.

Unlike the standard simplex and its faces in the type A case, this collection of generators is not invariant under the action of the corresponding Coxeter group. However, using the set of generators in the previous theorem, we are able to find a different collection of generators that is invariant under the action of
{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
, at the cost of including twice as many full-dimensional polytopes.

Theorem
6.11
.

Every type B generalized permutahedron in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be written uniquely as a signed Minkowski sum of the simplices
\Delta^{0}_{S}
for involution-exclusive subsets
S
.

This document is organized as follows. We review McMullen’s construction in Section
2
.
In Section
3
, we recall the definition of the Tits algebra of a hyperplane arrangement and some of its representation theoretic properties. Characteristic elements and Eulerian idempotents are also reviewed in this section. In Section
4
, we begin the study of the polytope algebra of deformations of a zonotope as a module over the Tits algebra of the corresponding hyperplane arrangement, which is the central construction for this paper. We study the polytope algebra of generalized permutahedra in Section
5
. In particular, we provide a conjectural basis of simultaneous eigenvectors for the action of the
Adams element
and positive dilations on the module
\Pi(\pi_{d})
. Section
6
contains the analogous results for type B. We also give explicit sets of
signed Minkowski generators
for type B generalized permutahedra; one shows that the lower bound on the number of full-dimensional polytopes in such a collection is tight, and the other is invariant under the action of
{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
. In Section
7
we prove that the valuation and translation invariance relations are compatible with the Hopf monoid structure of
GP
. Section
8
contains some final remarks and questions. We begin with some preliminaries.

1.1.
Preliminaries

Let
V
be a real vector space of dimension
d
endowed with an inner product
\langle\,\cdot\,,\cdot\,\rangle
, and let
{\bf 0}\in V
denote its zero vector.
For a polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq V
and a vector
v\in V
, let
{\mathfrak{p}}_{v}
denote the face of
{\mathfrak{p}}
maximized in the direction
v
.
That is,

{\mathfrak{p}}_{v}:=\{p\in{\mathfrak{p}}\,:\,\langle p,v\rangle\geq\langle q,v\rangle\text{ for all }q\in{\mathfrak{p}}\}.

The (outer)
normal cone
of a face
{\mathfrak{f}}
of
{\mathfrak{p}}
is the polyhedral cone

N({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{p}}):=\{v\in V\,:\,{\mathfrak{f}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}_{v}\}=\overline{\{v\in V\,:\,{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{p}}_{v}\}},

and the
normal fan
of
{\mathfrak{p}}
is the collection

\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=\{N({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{p}})\,:\,{\mathfrak{f}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}\}

of all normal cones of faces of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
There is a natural order-reversing correspondence between faces of
{\mathfrak{p}}
and cones in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
For
F\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we let
{\mathfrak{p}}_{F}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}
denote the face whose normal cone is
F
.
That is
{\mathfrak{p}}_{F}={\mathfrak{p}}_{v}
for any
v
in the relative interior of
F
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}

Figure 1.2.
A 2-dimensional polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}
and two of its faces
{\mathfrak{p}}_{v},{\mathfrak{p}}_{w}
maximized in directions
v,w
, respectively.
On the right, the normal fan
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
and the normal cones corresponding to the faces
{\mathfrak{p}}_{v},{\mathfrak{p}}_{w}
of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Recall that a fan
\Sigma

refines

\Sigma^{\prime}
if every cone in
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a union of cones in
\Sigma
. We say that a polytope
{\mathfrak{q}}
is a
deformation
of
{\mathfrak{p}}
if
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
refines
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{q}}
.
The
Minkowski sum
of two polytopes
{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{q}}\subseteq V
is the polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}+{\mathfrak{q}}:=\{p+q\,:\,p\in{\mathfrak{p}},\,q\in{\mathfrak{q}}\}
.
We say that a polytope
{\mathfrak{q}}
is a
Minkowski summand
of
{\mathfrak{p}}
if
{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathfrak{q}}+{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
for some polytope
{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
.
The normal fan of
{\mathfrak{p}}+{\mathfrak{q}}
is the common refinement of
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Hence,
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
refines the normal fan of any of its Minkowski summands, and the Minkowski sum of deformations of
{\mathfrak{p}}
is again a deformation of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.

The
f
-polynomial of a
d
-dimensional polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}
is

f({\mathfrak{p}},z)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}f_{i}({\mathfrak{p}})z^{i},

where
f_{i}({\mathfrak{p}})
is the number of
i
-dimensional faces of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
The
h
-polynomial of
{\mathfrak{p}}
is defined by

h({\mathfrak{p}},z)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}h_{i}({\mathfrak{p}})z^{i}=f({\mathfrak{p}},z-1).

The sequences
(f_{0}({\mathfrak{p}}),\dots,f_{d}({\mathfrak{p}}))
and
(h_{0}({\mathfrak{p}}),\dots,h_{d}({\mathfrak{p}}))
are the
f
-vector and
h
-vector of
{\mathfrak{p}}
, respectively.
These polynomials behave nicely with respect to the Cartesian product of polytopes:

f({\mathfrak{p}}\times{\mathfrak{q}},z)=f({\mathfrak{p}},z)f({\mathfrak{q}},z)\qquad h({\mathfrak{p}}\times{\mathfrak{q}},z)=h({\mathfrak{p}},z)h({\mathfrak{q}},z),

where
{\mathfrak{p}}\times{\mathfrak{q}}=\{(p,q)\,:\,p\in{\mathfrak{p}},\,q\in{\mathfrak{q}}\}
.

2.
The polytope algebra

We briefly review the construction of McMullen’s polytope algebra
[
21
]
and its main properties. A hands on introduction to this topic can be found in the survey
[
14
]
.
The subalgebra relative to a fixed polytope
[
22
]
is studied at the end of this section.

2.1.
Definition and structure theorem

As an abelian group, the
polytope algebra

\Pi(V)
is generated by elements
[{\mathfrak{p}}]
, one for each polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq V
.
These generators satisfy the relations

(2.1)

[{\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}]+[{\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}]=[{\mathfrak{p}}]+[{\mathfrak{q}}],

whenever
{\mathfrak{p}}
,
{\mathfrak{q}}
and
{\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}
are polytopes; and

(2.2)

[{\mathfrak{p}}+\{t\}]=[{\mathfrak{p}}],

for any polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}
and translation vector
t\in V
.
These relations are referred as the
valuation property
and the
translation invariance property
, respectively.
The group
\Pi(V)
is endowed with a commutative product defined on generators by means of the Minkowski sum

[{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot[{\mathfrak{q}}]:=[{\mathfrak{p}}+{\mathfrak{q}}].

It readily follows from (
2.2
) that the class of a point
1:=[\{{\bf 0}\}]
is the unit of
\Pi(V)
.

For each scalar
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, the
dilation morphism

\delta_{\lambda}:\Pi(V)\rightarrow\Pi(V)
is defined on generators by

\delta_{\lambda}[{\mathfrak{p}}]=[\lambda{\mathfrak{p}}].

Recall that for any subset
S\subseteq V
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
\lambda S:=\{\lambda v\,:\,v\in S\}
.
One can easily verify that
\delta_{\lambda}
preserves the valuation (
2.1
) and translation invariance (
2.2
) relations, and that it defines a morphism of rings.
The main structural result for the polytope algebra is the following.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
21
, Theorem 1]
)
.

The commutative ring
\Pi(V)
is almost a graded
\mathbb{R}
-algebra, in the following sense:

i.

as an abelian group,
\Pi(V)
admits a direct sum decomposition

\Pi(V)=\Xi_{0}(V)\oplus\Xi_{1}(V)\oplus\cdots\oplus\Xi_{d}(V);

ii.

under multiplication,

\Xi_{r}(V)\cdot\Xi_{s}(V)=\Xi_{r+s}(V),

with
\Xi_{r}(V)=0
for
r>d
;

iii.

\Xi_{0}(V)\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and for
r=1,\dots,d
,
\Xi_{r}(V)
is a real vector space;

iv.

the product of elements in
\bigoplus_{r\geq 1}\Xi_{r}(V)
is bilinear.

v.

the dilations
\delta_{\lambda}
are algebra endomorphisms, and for
r=0,1,\dots,d
, if
x\in\Xi_{r}(V)
and
\lambda\geq 0
, then

\delta_{\lambda}x=\lambda^{r}x.

We discuss the definition of the graded components
\Xi_{r}(V)
below. The component
\Xi_{0}(V)
of degree
0
is simply the subring of
\Pi(V)
generated by
1
, and thus
\Xi_{0}(V)\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
Z_{1}
be the subgroup of
\Pi(V)
generated by all elements of the form
[{\mathfrak{p}}]-1
.
Observe that
\delta_{0}[{\mathfrak{p}}]=1
for every polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}
, so
Z_{1}=\ker\delta_{0}
is an ideal.
As an abelian group,
\Pi(V)
has a direct sum decomposition

\Pi(V)=\Xi_{0}(V)\oplus Z_{1}.

Moreover,
Z_{1}
is a nil ideal, since for any
k
-dimensional polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}
,

([{\mathfrak{p}}]-1)^{r}=0\quad\text{for }r>k.

This is
[
21
, Lemma 13]
. McMullen also shows that
Z_{1}
has the structure of a vector space (first over
\mathbb{Q}
and then over
\mathbb{R}
). Therefore, we can define inverse maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+Z_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\log}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}

by means of their usual power series.
In particular, we can define the
log-class
of a
k
-dimensional polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}
by

(2.3)

\log[{\mathfrak{p}}]:=\log(1+([{\mathfrak{p}}]-1))=\sum_{r=1}^{k}\dfrac{(-1)^{r-1}}{r}([{\mathfrak{p}}]-1)^{r}.

Using the exponential map, we recover
[{\mathfrak{p}}]
from
\log[{\mathfrak{p}}]
:

(2.4)

[{\mathfrak{p}}]=\sum_{r=0}^{k}\dfrac{1}{r!}(\log[{\mathfrak{p}}])^{r}.

The
\log
and
\exp
maps satisfy the standard properties of logarithms and exponentials. In particular,

(2.5)

\log[{\mathfrak{p}}+{\mathfrak{q}}]=\log([{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot[{\mathfrak{q}}])=\log[{\mathfrak{p}}]+\log[{\mathfrak{q}}].

Example 2.2
.

Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\in V
be nonzero vectors and let
{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}
denote the line segment
\Conv\{{\bf 0},v_{i}\}
.
Then,
\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}]=[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}]-1
.
We will see that the product
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}]\in\Xi_{k}(V)
is nonzero if and only if the collection
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
is linearly independent. See Figure
2.1
for an example with
k=3
.

Consider the polytope
{\mathfrak{z}}={\mathfrak{l}}_{1}+{\mathfrak{l}}_{1}+\dots+{\mathfrak{l}}_{k}
, a Minkowski sum of segments.
Using the logarithm property (
2.5
), we get

(\log[{\mathfrak{z}}])^{k}=(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}])^{k}=k!\prod_{i=1}^{k}\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}].

The last equality follows since
k!\prod\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}]
is the only square-free term in the expansion of
(\sum\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}])^{k}
, and
(\log[{\mathfrak{l}}])^{2}=([{\mathfrak{l}}]-1)^{2}=0
for any line segment
{\mathfrak{l}}
.
Finally,
(\log[{\mathfrak{z}}])^{k}\neq 0
if and only if
k\leq\dim({\mathfrak{z}})
, and
{\mathfrak{z}}
being the sum of
k
line segments has dimension at most
k
, with equality precisely when the vectors
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
are linearly independent.

Figure 2.1.
Vectors
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
lie in the same plane. The vectors
v_{1},v_{2}
are linearly independent and
\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{1}]\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}]
represents
the class of a half-open parallelogram
. In contrast, the product
\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{1}]\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}]\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{3}]
is zero.

For
r\geq 1
let
\Xi_{r}(V)
be the subgroup (or subspace) of
\Pi(V)
generated by elements of the form
(\log[{\mathfrak{p}}])^{r}
. The following result implies that the sum
\Pi(V)=\Xi_{0}(V)+\Xi_{1}(V)+\dots+\Xi_{d}(V)
is direct, and characterizes each graded component as the space of eigenvectors for the positive dilations
\delta_{\lambda}
.

Lemma 2.3

(
[
21
, Lemma 20]
)
.

Let
x\in\Pi(V)
and
\lambda>0
, with
\lambda\neq 1
.
Then,

(2.6)

x\in\Xi_{r}(V)\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad\delta_{\lambda}x=\lambda^{r}x.

It is clear by the definition of the graded components
\Xi_{r}(V)
that the class of a half-open parallelogram in Figure
2.1
is in
\Xi_{2}(V)
. We can also verify this using (
2.6
) with
\lambda=2
:

Convention 2.4
.

As in later work of McMullen
[
23
,
22
]
, we replace
\Xi_{0}(V)\cong\mathbb{Z}
with the tensor product
\Xi_{0}(V)_{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathbb{R}\otimes\Xi_{0}(V)\cong\mathbb{R}
to get a genuine graded
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\Pi(V)_{\mathbb{R}}:=\Xi_{0}(V)_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus Z_{1}
.
To simplify notation, we drop the subscript
\mathbb{R}
and sometimes wirte
\Xi_{r}
and
\Pi
instead of
\Xi_{r}(V)
and
\Pi(V)_{\mathbb{R}}
.

For an arbitrary vector
v\in V
, we can define a
maximization operator

{\mathfrak{p}}\mapsto{\mathfrak{p}}_{v}
on the space of all polytopes
{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq V
.
The next result shows that it induces a well-defined map on
\Pi(V)
.

Theorem 2.5

(
[
21
, Theorem 7]
)
.

The map
{\mathfrak{p}}\mapsto{\mathfrak{p}}_{v}
induces an endomorphism
x\mapsto x_{v}
on
\Pi(V)
,
defined on generators by

[{\mathfrak{p}}]\mapsto[{\mathfrak{p}}]_{v}:=[{\mathfrak{p}}_{v}].

This endomorphism commutes with nonnegative dilations.

In particular, the morphism
x\mapsto x_{v}
restricts to each graded component
\Xi_{r}
.

Lastly, the
Euler map

x\mapsto x^{*}
is the linear operator defined on generators by

(2.7)

[{\mathfrak{p}}]^{*}=\sum_{{\mathfrak{q}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}}(-1)^{\dim({\mathfrak{q}})}[{\mathfrak{q}}].

The sum runs over all nonempty faces
{\mathfrak{q}}
of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Up to a sign, the element
[{\mathfrak{p}}]^{*}
corresponds to
the class of the interior
of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Theorem 2.6

(
[
21
, Theorem 2]
)
.

The Euler map is an involutory automorphism of
\Pi(V)
.
Moreover, for
x\in\Xi_{r}(V)
and
\lambda<0
,

\delta_{\lambda}x=\lambda^{r}x^{*}.

2.2.
Subalgebra relative to a fixed polytope

Fix a polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq V
.
The
subalgebra relative to
{\mathfrak{p}}
, denoted
\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})
, is the subalgebra of
\Pi(V)
generated by classes
[{\mathfrak{q}}]
of deformations
{\mathfrak{q}}
of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
It is worth pointing out that if
{\mathfrak{q}},{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
are deformations of
{\mathfrak{p}}
such that
{\mathfrak{q}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
is a polytope, then both
{\mathfrak{q}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathfrak{q}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
are deformations of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
This follows since, in this case,
{\mathfrak{q}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}+{\mathfrak{q}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}={\mathfrak{q}}+{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
and Minkowski summands of a deformation of
{\mathfrak{p}}
are again deformations of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Thus, the valuation property (
2.1
) is not introducing classes of new polytopes to
\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})
.

Remark 2.7
.

McMullen
[
22
]
originally defined
\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})
as the subalgebra generated by the classes of Minkowski summands of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
The following result of Shephard
[
18
, Section 15.2.7]
implies that both definitions are equivalent:
a polytope
{\mathfrak{q}}
is a deformation of
{\mathfrak{p}}
if and only if for small enough
\lambda>0
,
\lambda{\mathfrak{q}}
is a Minkowski summand of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.

The relations between
[{\mathfrak{q}}]
and
\log[{\mathfrak{q}}]
in (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) show that
\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})
is generated by homogeneous elements.
Thus, the grading of
\Pi(V)
induces a grading of
\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})
.
We let
\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{p}})=\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})\cap\Xi_{r}(V)
denote the component of
\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})
in degree
r
.
Unlike the full algebra
\Pi(V)
, the subalgebra
\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})
has finite dimension.
McMullen described the dimension of the graded components of
\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})
when
{\mathfrak{p}}
is a simple polytope.

Theorem 2.8

(
[
22
, Theorem 6.1]
)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{p}}
be a
d
-dimensional simple polytope.
Then,

\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{p}}))=h_{r}({\mathfrak{p}})

for
r=0,1,\dots,d
.

Let
{\mathfrak{f}}
be a face of
{\mathfrak{p}}
and
v\in\relint\big(N({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{p}})\big)
.
The maximization operator
x\mapsto x_{v}
defines a morphism of graded algebras

(2.8)

\psi_{\mathfrak{f}}:\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})\rightarrow\Pi({\mathfrak{f}})

that only depends on the face
{\mathfrak{f}}
and not on the particular choice of
v\in\relint\big(N({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{p}})\big)
.

First observe that this map is well defined; that is,
[{\mathfrak{q}}_{v}]\in\Pi({\mathfrak{f}})
for every generator
[{\mathfrak{q}}]
of
\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})
.
Indeed, if
{\mathfrak{q}}
is a summand of
{\mathfrak{p}}
, say
{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathfrak{q}}+{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
, then

{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{p}}_{v}={\mathfrak{q}}_{v}+{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}_{v},

so
{\mathfrak{q}}_{v}
is a Minkowski summand of
{\mathfrak{f}}
.
Moreover, since the normal fan of
{\mathfrak{p}}
refines that of
{\mathfrak{q}}
, then
{\mathfrak{q}}_{w}={\mathfrak{q}}_{v}
for any other
w\in\relint\big(N({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{p}})\big)
.
Therefore the morphism (
2.8
) only depends on
{\mathfrak{f}}
.

Theorem 2.9

(
[
22
, Theorem 2.4]
)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{p}}
be a simple polytope and
{\mathfrak{f}}
a face of
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Then, the morphism
\psi_{\mathfrak{f}}
is surjective.

3.
The Tits algebra of a linear hyperplane arrangement

3.1.
Basic definitions

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a linear hyperplane arrangement in
V
.
An arbitrary intersection of hyperplanes in
\mathcal{A}
is a
flat
of the arrangement.
The set
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]
of flats of
\mathcal{A}
is a lattice with maximum
\top:=V
and minimum
\perp
, the intersection of all hyperplanes in
\mathcal{A}
.
For a flat
\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]
, the
arrangement under
\mathrm{X}
is the following hyperplane arrangement in ambient space
\mathrm{X}
:

\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{X}}=\{{\mathrm{H}}\cap\mathrm{X}\,:\,{\mathrm{H}}\in\mathcal{A},\,\mathrm{X}\not\subseteq{\mathrm{H}}\}.

The
characteristic polynomial
of
\mathcal{A}
is

\chi(\mathcal{A},t):=\sum_{\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]}\,\mu(\mathrm{X},\top)\,t^{\dim(\mathrm{X})},

where
\mu(\mathrm{X},\top)
denotes the Möbius function of
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]
.
It is a monic polynomial of degree
\dim(V)
.

The hyperplanes in
\mathcal{A}
split
V
into a collection
\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]
of polyhedral cones called
faces
of
\mathcal{A}
.
Explicitly, the complement in
V
of the union of hyperplanes in
\mathcal{A}
is the disjoint union of open subsets of
V
; and
\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]
is the collection of the closures of these regions together with all their faces.

\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]
is a poset under containment, its maximal elements are called
chambers
.
The
central face

O
is the minimum element of
\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]
, it coincides with the minimal flat
\perp
of the arrangement.
See Figure
3.1
for an example.

The
support
of a face
F
is the smallest flat
\supp(F)
containing it.
Equivalently, it is the linear span of
F
.
The support map

(3.1)

\supp:\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]\to\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]

is surjective and order preserving.

Figure 3.1.
A 2-dimensional arrangement
\mathcal{A}
together with
its poset of faces (left) and lattice of flats (right).
The Möbius function of the lattice of flats is shown in blue.
The characteristic polynomial of
\mathcal{A}
is
\chi(\mathcal{A},t)=t^{2}-3t+2
.

3.2.
The Tits algebra and characteristic elements

The set
\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]
has the structure of a monoid under the
Tits product
illustrated in Figure
3.2
.
The product of two faces
F
and
G
, denoted
FG
, is the first face you encounter after moving a small positive distance from an interior point of
F
to an interior point of
G
.
The unit of this product is the central face
O\in\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]
.
The
Tits algebra
of
\mathcal{A}
is the monoid algebra
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]:=\mathbb{R}\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]
.
See
[
5
, Chapters 1 and 9]
for more details.
We let
\mathtt{H}_{F}
denote the basis element of
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
associated with the face
F
of
\mathcal{A}
.

Figure 3.2.
Product of faces in arrangements in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(left) and
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(right).
The second arrangement has been intersected with a sphere around the origin, its central face is not visible.

We view
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]
as a commutative monoid with the join operation
\vee
for the product.
This makes the support map (
3.1
) a morphism of monoids.
We let
\mathtt{H}_{\mathrm{X}}
denote the basis element of
\mathbb{R}\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]
associated to the flat
\mathrm{X}
of
\mathcal{A}
, so that
\mathtt{H}_{\mathrm{X}}\cdot\mathtt{H}_{\mathrm{Y}}=\mathtt{H}_{\mathrm{X}\vee\mathrm{Y}}
.

A result of Solomon
[
28
, Theorem 1]
shows that the monoid algebra
\mathbb{R}\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]
is split-semisimple.
This rests on the fact that the unique complete system of orthogonal idempotents for
\mathbb{R}\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]
consists of elements
\mathtt{Q}_{\mathrm{X}}
uniquely determined by

(3.2)

\mathtt{H}_{\mathrm{X}}=\sum_{\mathrm{Y}:\,\mathrm{Y}\geq\mathrm{X}}\mathtt{Q}_{\mathrm{Y}}\quad\text{or equivalently}\quad\mathtt{Q}_{\mathrm{X}}=\sum_{\mathrm{Y}:\,\mathrm{Y}\geq\mathrm{X}}\mu(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})\mathtt{H}_{\mathrm{Y}}.

In particular,
\mathbb{R}\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]
is the maximal split-semisimple quotient of
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
via the support map and the simple modules of
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
are indexed by flats.
The character
\chi_{\mathrm{X}}
of the simple module associated with the flat
\mathrm{X}
evaluated on an element

w=\sum_{F}w^{F}\mathtt{H}_{F}

of
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
yields

(3.3)

\chi_{\mathrm{X}}(w)=\sum_{F:\,\supp(F)\leq\mathrm{X}}w^{F}.

Let
t
be a fixed scalar.
An element
w
of the Tits algebra is
characteristic of parameter
t
if for each flat
\mathrm{X}

\chi_{\mathrm{X}}(w)=t^{\dim(\mathrm{X})},

with
\chi_{\mathrm{X}}(w)
as in (
3.3
).
Characteristic elements determine the characteristic polynomial of the arrangement and also determine the characteristic polynomial of the arrangements under each flat.
See
[
5
, Section 12.4]
and
[
2
]
for more information.

3.3.
Eulerian idempotents and diagonalization

An
Eulerian family
of
\mathcal{A}
is a collection of idempotent and mutually orthogonal elements
\{\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}\}_{\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]}\subseteq\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
of the form

\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}=\sum_{F\,:\,\supp(F)\geq\mathrm{X}}a^{F}\mathtt{H}_{F},

with
a^{F}\neq 0
for at least one
F
with
\supp(F)=\mathrm{X}
. It follows that
\{\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}\}_{\mathrm{X}}
is a complete system of primitive orthogonal idempotents and that
\supp(\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}})=\mathtt{Q}_{\mathrm{X}}

[
5
, Theorem 11.20]
.
That is,

\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{Y}}=\begin{cases}\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}&\text{if }\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{Y},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\qquad\qquad\qquad\sum_{\mathrm{X}}\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}=\mathtt{H}_{O},

and
\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}
cannot be written as the sum of two non-trivial idempotents.

Example 3.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
be the coordinate arrangement in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The following is an Eulerian family of
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
.
Observe that in this example only faces in the first quadrant have non-zero coefficients.

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.94 0 C 2.94 1.62 1.62 2.94 0 2.94 C -1.62 2.94 -2.94 1.62 -2.94 0 C -2.94 -1.62 -1.62 -2.94 0 -2.94 C 1.62 -2.94 2.94 -1.62 2.94 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{18.13731pt}{19.86229pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}

A characteristic element
w
of
non-critical
1
1

1

t\in\mathbb{R}
is non-critical if it is not a root of
\chi(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{X}},t)
for any flat
\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]
.
parameter
t
uniquely determines an Eulerian family
\mathtt{E}=\{\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}\}_{\mathrm{X}}
, which satisfies

(3.4)

w=\sum_{\mathrm{X}}t^{\dim(\mathrm{X})}\rm E_{\mathrm{X}}.

This is a consequence of
[
5
, Propositions 11.9, 12.59]
.
It follows that the action of such a characteristic elements
w
on any
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
-module
M
is diagonalizable.

Let
M
be a (right)
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
-module,
w\in\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
be a characteristic element of non-critical parameter
t
and
\{\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}\}_{\mathrm{X}}
be the corresponding Eulerian family. Then, we have a decomposition

(3.5)

M=\bigoplus_{\mathrm{X}}M\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}

of vector spaces. Expression (
3.4
) shows that
w
acts on
M\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}
by multiplication by
t^{\dim(\mathrm{X})}
. We define

\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(M):=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(M\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}).

The kernel of the support map
\supp
is precisely the radical of
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]

[
5
, Proposition 9.22]
. Consequently, the character
\chi_{M}:\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
of
M
factors through
\mathbb{R}\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\chi}_{M}}

Thus,
\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(M)=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(M\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}})=\chi_{M}(\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}})=\overline{\chi}_{M}(\mathtt{Q}_{\mathrm{X}})
is independent of the characteristic element
w
. Furthermore, using relations (
3.2
) and the linearity of
\overline{\chi}_{M}
we deduce

(3.6)

\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(M)=\overline{\chi}_{M}(\mathtt{Q}_{\mathrm{X}})=\sum_{\mathrm{Y}\geq\mathrm{X}}\mu(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})\overline{\chi}_{M}(\mathtt{H}_{\mathrm{Y}})\\
=\sum_{\mathrm{Y}\geq\mathrm{X}}\mu(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})\chi_{M}(\mathtt{H}_{F_{\mathrm{Y}}})=\sum_{\mathrm{Y}\geq\mathrm{X}}\mu(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(M\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F_{\mathrm{Y}}}),

where
F_{\mathrm{Y}}\in\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]
is such that
\supp(F_{\mathrm{Y}})=\mathrm{Y}
. The last equality follows since
\mathtt{H}_{F_{\mathrm{Y}}}
is an idempotent element, and thus
\chi_{M}(\mathtt{H}_{F_{\mathrm{Y}}})=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(M\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F_{\mathrm{Y}}})
.

Moreover, the number of composition factors
M_{i+1}/M_{i}
isomorphic to the simple module indexed by
\mathrm{X}
in a composition series

0\subset M_{1}\subset M_{2}\subset\dots\subset M_{k}=M

of
M
is precisely
\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(M)
.
See
[
5
, Section 9.5 and Theorem D.37]
for details.

4.
The polytope algebra as a module

Fix a hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{A}
in
V
.
Take a normal vector
v_{\mathrm{H}}
for each hyperplane
{\mathrm{H}}\in\mathcal{A}
, and consider the zonotope (Minkowski sum of segments):

(4.1)

{\mathfrak{z}}=\sum_{{\mathrm{H}}}\Conv\{{\bf 0},v_{\mathrm{H}}\}.

Its normal fan
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{z}}
coincides with the collection of faces
\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]
of the arrangement
\mathcal{A}
.
We say that a polytope
{\mathfrak{q}}
is a
generalized zonotope
of
\mathcal{A}
it is a deformation of
{\mathfrak{z}}
.

We now consider the algebra
\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})
introduced in Section
2.2
.
It is generated by the classes of generalized zonotopes of
\mathcal{A}
.
It only depends on the arrangement
\mathcal{A}
and not on the particular choice of normal vectors
v_{\mathrm{H}}
.
We start with a simple yet interesting result.
Recall the morphism
\psi_{\mathfrak{f}}:\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})\rightarrow\Pi({\mathfrak{f}})
defined for every face
{\mathfrak{f}}\leq{\mathfrak{z}}
in (
2.8
), it sends the class
[{\mathfrak{q}}]
of a generalized zonotope of
\mathcal{A}
to
[{\mathfrak{q}}_{v}]
where
v\in V
is any vector such that
{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{z}}_{v}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
{\mathfrak{z}}
be a zonotope, and
{\mathfrak{f}}
a face of
{\mathfrak{z}}
. Then, the morphism
\psi_{\mathfrak{f}}
is surjective.

Proof.

Let
{\mathfrak{f}}
be a face of
{\mathfrak{z}}
,
F\in\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]
be its normal cone, and
\mathrm{X}=\supp(F)
be the flat orthogonal to
{\mathfrak{f}}
.
It follows from (
4.1
) that
{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{z}}_{F}
is a translate of

{\mathfrak{z}}_{\mathrm{X}}:=\sum_{{\mathrm{H}}\,:\,{\mathrm{H}}\supseteq\mathrm{X}}\Conv\{{\bf 0},v_{\mathrm{H}}\}.

In particular,
{\mathfrak{f}}
is a Minkowski summand of
{\mathfrak{z}}
.
Being a Minkowski summand is a transitive relation.
Hence, any generator
[{\mathfrak{q}}]
of
\Pi({\mathfrak{f}})
is also in
\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})
.
That is,
\Pi({\mathfrak{f}})
is a subalgebra of
\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})
.
Moreover, if
v\in\relint(F)
and
{\mathfrak{q}}
is a Minkowski summand of
{\mathfrak{f}}
, then
{\mathfrak{q}}_{v}={\mathfrak{q}}
.
Therefore, the composition

\Pi({\mathfrak{f}})\hookrightarrow\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})\xrightarrow{\psi_{\mathfrak{f}}}\Pi({\mathfrak{f}})

is the identity map.
Consequently, the morphism
\psi_{\mathfrak{f}}
is surjective.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

Compare with Theorem
2.9
and note that we do not assume the zonotope
{\mathfrak{z}}
to be simple.
For an arbitrary polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}
and a face
{\mathfrak{f}}
of
{\mathfrak{p}}
, there is no natural morphism
\Pi({\mathfrak{f}})\rightarrow\Pi({\mathfrak{p}})
, unlike in the previous case.
This is a particular property of zonotopes. Indeed, a polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}
is a zonotope if and only if every face
{\mathfrak{f}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}
is a Minkowski summand of
{\mathfrak{p}}
, see
[
11
, Proposition 2.2.14]
for a proof.

Let
F
be a face of
\mathcal{A}
and
{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{z}}_{F}
the corresponding face of
{\mathfrak{z}}
.
We define right multiplication by the basis element
\mathtt{H}_{F}\in\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
on
\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})

as the projection
\psi_{\mathfrak{f}}:\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})\rightarrow\Pi({\mathfrak{f}})\subseteq\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})
.

Theorem 4.3
.

The algebra
\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})
is a right
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
-module under the action above.
Explicitly, for a generator
[{\mathfrak{q}}]
of
\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})
and a basis element
\mathtt{H}_{F}
of
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
,

[{\mathfrak{q}}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}:=[{\mathfrak{q}}_{v}],\qquad\text{where }v\in\relint(F).

Moreover, each graded component
\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}})
is a
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
-submodule and the action of basis elements
\{\mathtt{H}_{F}\}_{F}
on
\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})
is by
(
graded
)
algebra endomorphisms.

Proof.

The zero vector belongs to the central face
O
, so the action is clearly unital.
Associativity follows from the following fact about polytopes
[
18
, Section 3.1.5]
.
If
{\mathfrak{q}}\subseteq V
is a polytope and
v,w\in V
, then

({\mathfrak{q}}_{v})_{w}={\mathfrak{q}}_{v+\lambda w}

for any small enough
\lambda>0
.
Similarly, the definition of the Tits product is such that if
v\in\relint(F)
and
w\in\relint(G)
, then
v+\lambda w\in\relint(FG)
for any small enough
\lambda>0
.
Hence,

([{\mathfrak{q}}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F})\cdot\mathtt{H}_{G}=[({\mathfrak{q}}_{v})_{w}]=[{\mathfrak{q}}_{v+\lambda w}]=[{\mathfrak{q}}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{FG}.

It follows that this product gives
\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})
the structure of a right
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
-module.

The second statement follows directly from Theorem
2.5
and the characterization of the graded components
\Xi_{r}
in (
2.6
).
Indeed, for any
x\in\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}})
and
\lambda>0
,

\delta_{\lambda}(x\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F})=\delta_{\lambda}(x)\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}=\lambda^{r}x\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}=\lambda^{r}(x\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}),

thus
x\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}\in\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}})
.
∎

4.1.
Simultaneous diagonalization

Let
\lambda>0
and let
w\in\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
be a characteristic element of non-critical parameter
t
.
We know that the dilation morphism
\delta_{\lambda}
and the action of
w
are diagonalizable.
Moreover, since
\delta_{\lambda}
and the action of
w
commute, they are simultaneously diagonalizable.
A natural question is to determine the eigenvalues of
\delta_{\lambda}
and of the action of
w
in their simultaneous eigenspaces.
We completely answer this question in the case of the Coxeter arrangements of type A and B in the next two sections.
The following result holds in the general case.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
x\in\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})
be a
(
nonzero
)
simultaneous eigenvector for
\delta_{\lambda}
and
w
with eigenvalues
\lambda^{r}
and
t^{k}
, respectively.
Then,
r+k\leq d
.

Proof.

Let
\{\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}\}_{\mathrm{X}}
be the Eulerian family associated to
w
.
Using the characterization of the graded components
\Xi_{r}
as the eigenspaces of
\delta_{\lambda}
, and the decomposition of
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
-modules in (
3.5
), we deduce that the common eigenspace for
\delta_{\lambda}
and
w
with the given eigenvalues is
\bigoplus_{\mathrm{X}}\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}
, where the sum is over all
k
-dimensional flats of
\mathcal{A}
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
x\in\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}
for a single
k
-dimensional flat
\mathrm{X}
.

Proposition
4.1
implies that
\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}})\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}=\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}}_{\mathrm{Y}})
, where
F\in\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]
is any face of support
\mathrm{Y}
.
Hence, formula (
3.6
) yields

(4.2)

\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}}))=\sum_{\mathrm{Y}\geq\mathrm{X}}\mu(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}}_{\mathrm{Y}})).

If
k>d-r
, then
\dim({\mathfrak{z}}_{\mathrm{Y}})=d-\dim(\mathrm{Y})\leq d-k<r
and
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}}_{\mathrm{Y}}))=0
for any
\mathrm{Y}
in the sum.
So, in this case,
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}})=\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}}))=0
.
This contradicts that
x\in\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}
is a nonzero element.
Therefore,
r+k\leq d
.
∎

If in addition
\mathcal{A}
is a simplicial arrangement, like in the case of reflection arrangements, then
{\mathfrak{z}}
and each of its faces are simple polytopes.
In that case, Theorem
2.8
allows us to replace
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}}_{\mathrm{Y}}))
by
h_{r}({\mathfrak{z}}_{\mathrm{Y}})
in expression (
4.2
).
Multiplying by
z^{r}
and taking the sum over all values of
r
, we obtain

(4.3)

\sum_{r}\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{z}}))z^{r}=\sum_{\mathrm{Y}\geq\mathrm{X}}\mu(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})h({\mathfrak{z}}_{\mathrm{Y}},z).

4.2.
First example: the cube and the coordinate arrangement

Let
{\mathcal{C}}_{d}
be the coordinate arrangement in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
It consists of the
d
coordinate hyperplanes
x_{i}=0
for
i=1,\dots,d
.
We identify the lattice of flats
\mathcal{L}[{\mathcal{C}}_{d}]
with the (opposite) boolean lattice
2^{[d]}
in the following manner:

S\subseteq[d]\longleftrightarrow\mathrm{X}_{S}:=\bigcap_{i\in S}\{x\,:\,x_{i}=0\}.

Observe that
\mathrm{X}_{S}\leq\mathrm{X}_{T}
if and only if
T\subseteq S
, and in this case
\mu_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathrm{X}_{S},\mathrm{X}_{T})=\mu_{2^{[d]}}(T,S)=(-1)^{|S\setminus T|}
.

The
d
-cube
{\mathfrak{c}}_{d}=[0,1]^{d}
a zonotope of
{\mathcal{C}}_{d}
.
It is the Minkowski sum of the
d
line segments
{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}:=\Conv\{{\bf 0},e_{i}\}
for
i=1,\dots,d
.
It is a simple polytope with
h
-polynomial
h({\mathfrak{c}}_{d},z)=(1+z)^{d}
.
Furthermore, for any
S\subseteq[d]
we have

({\mathfrak{c}}_{d})_{\mathrm{X}_{S}}=\sum_{i\in S}{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}\cong{\mathfrak{c}}_{|S|}.

Let us consider the right
\mathsf{\Sigma}[{\mathcal{C}}_{d}]
-module
\Pi({\mathfrak{c}}_{d})
.
For a flat
\mathrm{X}_{S}
, formula (
4.3
) yields

\sum_{r}\eta_{\mathrm{X}_{S}}(\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{c}}_{d}))z^{r}=\sum_{T\subseteq S}\mu(\mathrm{X}_{S},\mathrm{X}_{T})h({\mathfrak{c}}_{|T|},z)=\sum_{T\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S\setminus T|}(1+z)^{|T|}=z^{|S|}.

Hence,

\eta_{\mathrm{X}_{S}}(\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{c}}_{d}))=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }|S|=r,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

In particular, a series decomposition of
\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{c}}_{d})
contains exactly one copy of the simple module indexed by
\mathrm{X}_{S}
for every
S\in{[d]\choose r}
.

Let us now consider the characteristic element
\gamma_{t}\in\mathsf{\Sigma}[{\mathcal{C}}_{d}]
introduced in
[
2
, Section 5.3]
for
t\neq 1
.
It is defined by

\gamma_{t}=\sum_{F}\gamma_{t}^{F}\mathtt{H}_{F},\quad\text{where}\quad\gamma_{t}^{F}=\begin{cases}(t-1)^{\dim(F)}&\text{ if\penalty\ $F$ lies in the first orthant,}\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

For each
S\subseteq[d]
, let
F_{S}
be the intersection of the first orthant with
\mathrm{X}_{S}
, it is a face of
{\mathcal{C}}_{d}
.
We have
\supp(F_{S})=\mathrm{X}_{S}
and
T\subseteq S
if and only if
F_{S}\leq F_{T}
.
A simple computation shows that the Eulerian family corresponding to the characteristic element
\gamma_{t}
is determined by

\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}_{S}}=\sum_{T\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S\setminus T|}\mathtt{H}_{F_{T}}.

In dimension 2, this is the Eulerian family in Example
3.1
.
For each
S\subseteq[d]
, define

y_{S}=\prod_{i\in S}\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}]\in\Pi({\mathfrak{c}}_{d}).

Example
2.2
shows that
y_{S}
is a nonzero element of
\Pi({\mathfrak{c}}_{d})
.

We claim that
\{y_{S}\}_{S\subseteq[d]}
is a basis of simultaneous eigenvectors of
\Pi({\mathfrak{c}}_{d})
.
Explicitly,
y_{S}
is an eigenvector for the action of
\gamma_{t}
of eigenvalue
t^{d-|S|}
, and for the action of
\delta_{\lambda}
of eigenvalue
\lambda^{|S|}
(
\lambda>0
).
The second statement is clear, since
\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}]\in\Xi_{1}({\mathfrak{c}}_{d})
.
Moreover, using that
\log[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}]=[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}]-1
, we have

y_{S}=\prod_{i\in S}([{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}]-1)=\sum_{T\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S\setminus T|}[{\mathfrak{c}}_{\mathrm{X}_{T}}].

On the other hand, observe that

[{\mathfrak{c}}_{\mathrm{X}_{S}}]\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}_{S}}=\sum_{T\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S\setminus T|}[{\mathfrak{c}}_{\mathrm{X}_{S}}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F_{T}}=\sum_{T\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S\setminus T|}[{\mathfrak{c}}_{\mathrm{X}_{T}}]=y_{S}.

Therefore,

y_{S}\in\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{c}}_{d})\cap(\Pi({\mathfrak{c}}_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}_{S}})=\Xi_{r}({\mathfrak{c}}_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}_{S}}.

The claim follows since
\dim(\mathrm{X}_{S})=d-|S|
.

4.3.
The zonotope module of a product of arrangements

The Cartesian product of two arrangements
\mathcal{A}
in
V
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
in
W
is the following collection of hyperplanes in
V\oplus W
:

\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\{{\mathrm{H}}\oplus W\,:\,{\mathrm{H}}\in\mathcal{A}\}\cup\{V\oplus{\mathrm{H}}\,:\,{\mathrm{H}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\}.

One can easily verify that
\Sigma[\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]\cong\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]\times\Sigma[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]
as monoids.
Hence,
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]\cong\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]\otimes\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]
.
In fact, it is also true that

\Pi({\mathfrak{z}}\times{\mathfrak{z}}^{\prime})\cong\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})\otimes\Pi({\mathfrak{z}}^{\prime}),

where
{\mathfrak{z}}
and
{\mathfrak{z}}^{\prime}
are zonotopes of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, respectively, and therefore
{\mathfrak{z}}\times{\mathfrak{z}}^{\prime}
is a zonotope of
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
Indeed, every generalized zonotope of
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is the Cartesian product of generalized zonotopes of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
The corresponding isomorphism is induced by

\begin{array}[]{CCC}\Pi({\mathfrak{z}})\otimes\Pi({\mathfrak{z}}^{\prime})&\rightarrow&\Pi({\mathfrak{z}}\times{\mathfrak{z}}^{\prime})\\
{[{\mathfrak{p}}]}\otimes[{\mathfrak{q}}]&\mapsto&[{\mathfrak{p}}\times{\mathfrak{q}}]\end{array}

The fact that this map is well-defined and a morphism of
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]
-modules follows from the ideas in Section
7.2
.

5.
The module of generalized permutahedra

Generalized permutahedra are the deformations of the standard permutahedron
\pi_{d}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Edmonds first introduced them under a different name in
[
15
]
, where he studied their relation to submodular functions and optimization.
For a thorough study of the combinatorics of these polytopes, see
[
1
,
26
,
25
]
.

In this section, we study the algebra
\Pi(\pi_{d})
of generalized permutahedra and its structure as a module over the Tits algebra of the braid arrangement
\mathcal{A}_{d}
.
We begin with a brief review of the braid arrangement, its relation with the symmetric group, and some statistics on permutations.

5.1.
The braid arrangement

The braid arrangement
\mathcal{A}_{d}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
consists of the
diagonal
hyperplanes
x_{i}=x_{j}
for
1\leq i<j\leq d
.
Its central face is the line perpendicular to the hyperplane
x_{1}+\cdots+x_{d}=0
. Intersecting
\mathcal{A}_{d}
with this hyperplane and a sphere around the origin we obtain the
Coxeter complex of type
A_{d-1}
. The pictures below show the cases
d=3
and
4
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}

Flats and faces of
\mathcal{A}_{d}
are in one-to-one correspondence with set partitions and set compositions of
[d]:=\{1,2,\dots,d\}
, respectively.
We proceed to review this correspondence.

A
weak set partition
of a finite set
I
is a collection
\mathrm{X}=\{S_{1},\dots,S_{k}\}
of pairwise disjoint subsets
S_{i}\subseteq I
such that
I=S_{1}\cup\dots\cup S_{k}
.
The subsets
S_{i}
are the
blocks
of
\mathrm{X}
.
A
set partition
is a weak set partition with no empty blocks.
We write
\mathrm{X}\vdash I
to denote that
\mathrm{X}
is a set partition of
I
.
Given a partition
\mathrm{X}\vdash[d]
, the corresponding flat of
\mathcal{A}_{d}
is the intersection of the hyperplanes
x_{a}=x_{b}
for all
a,b
that belong to the same block of
\mathrm{X}
, as illustrated in the following example for
d=8
:

x_{1}=x_{3},\quad x_{2}=x_{5}=x_{6}=x_{8}\qquad\longleftrightarrow\qquad\{13,2568,4,7\},

where we write
13
to abbreviate the set
\{1,3\}
,
2568
to abbreviate the set
\{2,5,6,8\}
and so on.
We use
\mathrm{X}
to denote both a flat of
\mathcal{A}_{d}
and the corresponding set partition of
[d]
.
Observe that
\dim(\mathrm{X})
is precisely the number of blocks of
\mathrm{X}
as a partition.
The partial order relation of
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}_{d}]
becomes the ordering by refinement of set partitions. That is,
\mathrm{X}\leq\mathrm{Y}
if the set partition
\mathrm{X}
is
refined by

\mathrm{Y}
. Recall that
\mathrm{X}
is refined by
\mathrm{Y}
if every block of
\mathrm{X}
is the union of some blocks in
\mathrm{Y}
. For instance,
\{12345678\}\leq\{13,2568,4,7\}\leq\{1,28,3,4,56,7\}
.

If
S\subseteq I
is a union of blocks of a partition
\mathrm{X}\vdash I
, we let
\mathrm{X}|_{S}\vdash S
denote the partition of
S
formed by the blocks of
\mathrm{X}
whose union is
S
.
Let
\mathrm{X}=\{S_{1},\dots,S_{k}\}\vdash[d]
be a partition.
Then, the choice of
\mathrm{Y}\geq\mathrm{X}
is equivalent to the choice of partitions
\mathrm{Y}|_{S_{i}}\vdash S_{i}
for each block of
\mathrm{X}
.
With
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{Y}
as above, the Möbius function of
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}_{d}]
is determined by

(5.1)

\mu(\perp,\mathrm{X})=(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mu(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})=\mu(\perp,\mathrm{Y}|_{S_{1}})\dots\mu(\perp,\mathrm{Y}|_{S_{k}}),

where in each factor,
\perp
denotes the minimum partition of
S_{i}
.

A
set composition
of
I
is an ordered set partition
F=(S_{1},\dots,S_{k})
.
We write
F\vDash I
to denote that
F
is a composition of
I
, and let
\supp(F)\vdash I
be the underlying (unordered) set partition.
Given a set composition
F\vDash[d]
, the corresponding face of
\mathcal{A}_{d}
is obtained by intersecting the hyperplanes
x_{a}=x_{b}
whenever
a,b
are in the same block of
F
, and the halfspaces
x_{a}\geq x_{b}
whenever the block containing
a
precedes the block containing
b
. For example,

x_{1}=x_{3}\geq x_{4}\geq x_{2}=x_{5}=x_{6}=x_{8}\geq x_{7}\qquad\longleftrightarrow\qquad(13,4,2568,7).

5.2.
The symmetric group and the Eulerian polynomial

The symmetric group
{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
is the group of
permutations

\sigma:[d]\rightarrow[d]
under composition.
It is the Coxeter group corresponding to the braid arrangement. It acts on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by permuting coordinates:

\sigma(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d})=(x_{\sigma(1)},x_{\sigma(2)},\dots,x_{\sigma(d)}).

For a permutation
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
, we let
\supp(\sigma)
denote the
subspace of points fixed by the action
of
\sigma
; it is a flat of
\mathcal{A}_{d}
.
In view of the identification between flats of
\mathcal{A}_{d}
and partitions of
[d]
,
\supp(\sigma)
can equivalently be defined as the partition of
[d]
into the disjoint cycles of
\sigma
.
For example, if in cycle notation
\sigma=({\bf 1}3)({\bf 2}6{\bf 5}8)(4)(7)
, then
\supp(\sigma)=\{13,2568,4,7\}
.

Recall that
i\in[d-1]
is a
descent
of
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
if
\sigma(i)>\sigma(i+1)
,
and
i\in[d-1]
is an
excedance
of
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
if
\sigma(i)>i
. Let
\des(\sigma)
and
\exc(\sigma)
denote the number of descents and excedances of
\sigma
, respectively. In the example above,
1,2,5
(in bold) are the excedances of
\sigma
, and we have
\exc(\sigma)=3
. We can similarly define descents and excedances for permutations of any set
S
with a total order
\prec
, we denote the corresponding statistics by
\des_{\prec}
and
\exc_{\prec}
.

It is a classical result that descents and excedances are
equidistributed
in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
.
That is,

A_{d,k}:=\big|\{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}\,:\,\des(\sigma)=k\}\big|=\big|\{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}\,:\,\exc(\sigma)=k\}\big|,

for all possible values of
k
.
Foata’s
fundamental transformation
provides a simple proof of this result.
The numbers
A_{d,k}
are the classical
Eulerian numbers
(OEIS:
A008292
).
The
Eulerian polynomial

A_{d}(z)
is:

A_{d}(z):=\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}A_{d,k}z^{k}=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}}z^{\exc(\sigma)}.

The exponential generating function for these polynomials was originally given by Euler himself:

(5.2)

A(z,x)=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}A_{d}(z)\dfrac{x^{d}}{d!}=\dfrac{z-1}{z-e^{x(z-1)}}.

See
[
16
, Section 3]
for a derivation of this formula.

Let
{\mathfrak{C}}(S)
the collection of
cyclic permutations
on a finite set
S
, and
{\mathfrak{C}}(d)={\mathfrak{C}}([d])
.
Given a permutation
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
and a block
S\in\supp(\sigma)
, the restriction
\sigma|_{S}
of
\sigma
to
S
is a cyclic permutation. For example, with
\sigma
as before and
S=\{2,5,6\}\in\supp(\sigma)
, we have
\sigma|_{S}=(265)\in{\mathfrak{C}}(\{2,5,6\})
. A very simple but important observation is that the number of excedances of
\sigma
can be computed by adding up the excedances in each cycle in its cycle decomposition. That is,
\exc(\sigma)=\sum_{S\in\supp(\sigma)}\exc(\sigma|_{S})
. The number of excedances in each cycle
\sigma|_{S}
is computed with respect to the natural order in
S\subseteq[d]
.

5.3.
The module Generalized permutahedra

The permutahedron
\pi_{d}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the convex hull of the
{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
-orbit the point
(1,2,\dots,d)
.
It is a zonotope of the braid arrangement
\mathcal{A}_{d}
and has dimension
d-1
.
Deformations of
\pi_{d}
are called
generalized permutahedra
.
We consider the module
\Pi(\pi_{d})
as in Section
4
. The main goal of this section will be to prove the following result.

Theorem 5.1
.

For any flat
\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}_{d}]
and
r=0,1,\dots,d-1
,

\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}(\pi_{d}))=\big|\{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}\,:\,\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X},\ \exc(\sigma)=r\}\big|.

Figure 5.1.
The permutahedron in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

The relation between
\Pi(\pi_{d})
and statistics on
{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
is via the
h
-polynomial of
\pi_{d}
.
Brenti
[
12
, Theorem 2.3]
showed that
h(\pi_{d},z)=A_{d}(z)
. Moreover, for a flat/partition
\mathrm{X}=\{S_{1},\dots,S_{k}\}
of
\mathcal{A}_{d}
, the face
(\pi_{d})_{\mathrm{X}}
is a translate of
\pi_{|S_{1}|}\times\dots\times\pi_{|S_{k}|}
, a product of lower-dimensional permutahedra. Thus,

(5.3)

h((\pi_{d})_{\mathrm{X}},z)=A_{|S_{1}|}(z)\cdot\ldots\cdot A_{|S_{k}|}(z).

Lemma
5.3
below is an essential ingredient in the proof of Theorem
5.1
. Its proof uses the Compositional Formula, for which a type B analog is proved in Proposition
6.3
.

Theorem 5.2

(The Compositional Formula
[
29
, Theorem 5.1.4]
)
.

Let

g(x)=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}g_{d}\dfrac{x^{d}}{d!}\qquad a(x)=\sum_{d\geq 1}a_{d}\dfrac{x^{d}}{d!}.

If

h(x)=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}h_{d}\dfrac{x^{d}}{d!!}\qquad\text{where}\qquad h_{d}=\sum_{\{S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{k}\}\vdash[d]}g_{k}a_{|S_{1}|}\dots a_{|S_{k}|},

then

h(x)=g(a(x)).

Lemma 5.3
.

For every
d\geq 1
,

(5.4)

\sum_{\mathrm{X}=\{S_{1},\dots,S_{k}\}\vdash[d]}\mu(\perp,\mathrm{X})A_{|S_{1}|}(z)\cdot\ldots\cdot A_{|S_{k}|}(z)=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{C}}(d)}z^{\exc(\sigma)}.

Proof.

We will show that the exponential generating function of both sides of (
5.4
) are equal to
\log(A(z,x))
, where
A(z,x)
is the generating function for the Eulerian polynomials in (
5.2
).

First, recall that if
\mathrm{X}=\{S_{1},\dots,S_{k}\}
, then
\mu(\perp,\mathrm{X})=(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!
.
Thus, a direct application of the Compositional Formula shows that the exponential generating function of the LHS of (
5.4
) is the composition of

\sum_{d\geq 1}(-1)^{d-1}(d-1)!\dfrac{x^{d}}{d!}=\log(1+x)\qquad\text{with}\qquad\sum_{d\geq 1}A_{d}(z)\dfrac{x^{d}}{d!}=A(z,x)-1,

which is precisely
\log(A(z,x))
.

On the other hand, grouping permutations with the same underlying partition
\supp(\sigma)
, we obtain

A(z,x)=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}\Big(\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}}z^{\exc(\sigma)}\Big)\dfrac{x^{d}}{d!}=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}\Big(\sum_{\mathrm{X}\vdash[d]}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}\\
\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X}\end{subarray}}z^{\exc(\sigma)}\Big)\Big)\dfrac{x^{d}}{d!}.

Since a permutation
\sigma
with
\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X}
is the product of cyclic permutations
\sigma_{S}\in{\mathfrak{C}}(S)
for each block
S\in\mathrm{X}
, and in this case
\exc(\sigma)=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{X}}\exc(\sigma_{S})
,

(5.5)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}\\
\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X}\end{subarray}}z^{\exc(\sigma)}=\prod_{S\in\mathrm{X}}\Big(\sum_{\sigma_{S}\in{\mathfrak{C}}(S)}z^{\exc(\sigma_{S})}\Big).

Thus, the Exponential Formula
[
29
, Corollary 5.1.6]
implies that

A(z,x)=\exp\bigg(\sum_{d\geq 1}\Big(\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{C}}(d)}z^{\exc(\sigma)}\Big)\dfrac{x^{d}}{d!}\bigg).

Taking logarithms on both sides yields the result.
∎

A small modification in the proof of the previous Lemma immediately gives the following result, which was first discovered by Brenti.

Corollary 5.4

(
[
13
, Proposition 7.3]
)
.

The following identity holds

1+\sum_{d\geq 1}\Big(\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}}t^{|\supp(\sigma)|}z^{\exc(\sigma)}\Big)\dfrac{x^{n}}{n!}=\exp(t\log(A(z,x)))=\left(\dfrac{z-1}{z-e^{x(z-1)}}\right)^{t}.

An analogous formula for the type B Coxeter group is described in Proposition
6.7
.
We are now ready to prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We will compute the values
\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}(\pi_{d}))
using formula (
4.3
),
which in this case reads

\sum_{r}\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}(\pi_{d}))z^{r}=\sum_{\mathrm{Y}\,:\,\mathrm{Y}\geq\mathrm{X}}\mu(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})h((\pi_{d})_{\mathrm{Y}},z).

Using (
5.1
) and (
5.3
), we can rewrite the expression above as

\sum_{r}\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}(\pi_{d}))z^{r}=\prod_{S\in\mathrm{X}}\Big(\sum_{\mathrm{Y}=\{T_{1},\dots,T_{\ell}\}\vdash S}\mu(\perp,\mathrm{Y})A_{|T_{1}|}(z)\cdot\ldots\cdot A_{|T_{\ell}|}(z)\Big).

Now, an application of Lemma
5.3
and relation (
5.5
) gives

\sum_{r}\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}(\pi_{d}))z^{r}=\prod_{S\in\mathrm{X}}\Big(\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{C}}(S)}z^{\exc(\sigma)}\Big)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}\\
\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X}\end{subarray}}z^{\exc(\sigma)}.

Finally, taking the coefficient of
z^{r}
on both sides of the last equality yields the result.
∎

Adding over all flats with the same dimension in Theorem
5.1
, we conclude the following.

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
w\in\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}_{d}]
be a characteristic element of non-critical parameter
t
and
\lambda>0
.
The dimension of the simultaneous eigenspace for
w
and
\delta_{\lambda}
with eigenvalues
t^{k}
and
\lambda^{r}
is

\big|\{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}\,:\,|\supp(\sigma)|=k,\ \exc(\sigma)=r\}\big|.

5.4.
Simultaneous-eigenbasis for the Adams element

Perhaps the most natural characteristic elements for the braid arrangement are the
Adams elements
, defined for any parameter
t
by:

\alpha_{t}=\sum_{F}{t\choose\dim(F)}\mathtt{H}_{F}.

It is invariant with respect to the action of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
, and its action on
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}_{d}]
-modules is closely related with the
convolution powers of the identity map
of a Hopf monoid, see
[
4
, Section 14.4]
.
The corresponding Eulerian idempotents are
[
5
, Theorem 12.75]

\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}=\dfrac{1}{\dim(\mathrm{X})!}\sum_{\supp(F)=\mathrm{X}}\sum_{G\geq F}\dfrac{(-1)^{\dim(G/F)}}{\deg(G/F)}\mathtt{H}_{G},

where
\dim(G/F)=\dim(G)-\dim(F)
and,

\deg(G/F)=\prod_{S\in F}\big|G|_{S}\big|.

\alpha_{t}=\begin{gathered}\hbox to83.43pt{\vbox to86.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.71376pt\lower-43.25372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}

Figure 5.2.
The Adams element
\alpha_{t}
and some of the associated Eulerian idempotents of the braid arrangement in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Theorem
5.1
suggest the existence of a natural basis for
\Xi_{r}(\pi_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}
indexed by permutations
\sigma
with
r
excedances and
\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X}
.
In this section we will construct a candidate for such basis.

The standard simplex
\Delta_{[d]}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the convex hull of the standard basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Similarly, for any nonempty subset
S\subseteq[d]
, we let
\Delta_{S}=\Conv\{e_{i}\,:\,i\in S\}
.

Theorem 5.6

(
[
6
,
26
]
)
.

Every generalized permutahedron
{\mathfrak{p}}
can be written uniquely as a
signed Minkowski sum
of the simplices
\{\Delta_{S}\,:\,S\subseteq[d]\}
.
That is, there is a unique choice of scalars
\{y_{S}\}_{S\subseteq[d]}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
such that

{\mathfrak{p}}+\sum_{y_{S}<0}|y_{S}|\Delta_{S}=\sum_{y_{S}>0}y_{S}\Delta_{S}.

Up to translation, this is equivalent to the following identity in
\Xi_{1}(\pi_{d})
:

\log[{\mathfrak{p}}]=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}y_{S}\log[\Delta_{S}].

Given that
\log[\Delta_{S}]=0
whenever
S
is a singleton, and that
[{\mathfrak{p}}]=[{\mathfrak{q}}]
if and only if
{\mathfrak{p}}
is a translate of
{\mathfrak{q}}
,
we conclude that
\{\log[\Delta_{S}]\,:\,S\subseteq[d],\,\big|S\big|\geq 2\}
is a linear basis for
\Xi_{1}(\pi_{d})
and therefore generate
\Pi(\pi_{d})
as an algebra.
This agrees with
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\Xi_{1}(\pi_{d}))=h_{1}(\pi_{d})=2^{d}-d-1
.

We will use a bijection between
increasing rooted forests
on
[d]
and permutations in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
.
An increasing rooted forest is a disjoint union of planar rooted trees where each child is larger than its parent and the children are in increasing order from left to right.
Given a rooted forest
t
, the corresponding permutation
\sigma(t)
is read as follows.
Each connected component of
t
corresponds to a cycle of
\sigma(t)
.
To form a cycle, traverse the corresponding tree counterclockwise and record a node the
second
time you pass by it
2
2

2

A similar bijection is described by Peter Luschny in this
OEIS entry
.
.

The inverse can be described inductively by writing each cycle with its minimum element in the last position, and using right to left minima.
We omit the details, but provide an example
\sigma\mapsto t(\sigma)
to illustrate the idea.

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 117.91 22.43 h 16.14 v 18.14 h -16.14 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 114.45 -9.07 h 23.06 v 18.14 h -23.06 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}

This bijection is such that the connected components of the forest
t(\sigma)
are the blocks of
\supp(\sigma)
.
Moreover, the number of leaves of
t(\sigma)
in
S\in\supp(\sigma)
is
\exc(\sigma|_{S})
(a tree consisting only of its root has zero leaves).
Consequently, the total number of leaves of
t(\sigma)
is
\exc(\sigma)
.

Let
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
be a permutation with
r
excedances and let
\mathrm{X}=\supp(\sigma)
.
For
1\leq i\leq r
, let
J_{i}
be the elements on the path from the
i^{th}
leaf of
t(\sigma)
to the root of the corresponding tree.
Define the element

(5.7)

x_{\sigma}=\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{r}\log[\Delta_{J_{i}}]\Big)\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}.

For instance, if
\sigma
is the permutation in (
5.6
), then

x_{\sigma}=\Big(\log[\Delta_{\{7,3,1\}}]\log[\Delta_{\{6,5,1\}}]\log[\Delta_{\{9,5,1\}}]\log[\Delta_{\{4,2\}}]\log[\Delta_{\{10,8,2\}}]\Big)\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}},

where
\mathrm{X}=\{1,3,5,6,7,9\},\{2,4,8,10\}
.

Conjecture 5.7
.

For fixed
\mathrm{X}\vdash[d]
and
r\leq d-\big|\mathrm{X}\big|
, the collection

\{x_{\sigma}\,:\,\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X},\ \exc(\sigma)=r\}

is a linear basis of
\Xi_{r}(\pi_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}
.

It follows from the definition (
5.7
) that
x_{\sigma}\in\Xi_{r}(\pi_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}
.
The content of the conjecture is that these elements are linearly independent.
Explicit computations show that this is the case for
d=2,3,4
.
Propositions
5.8
and
5.9
below prove the extremal cases
r=1
and
r=d-|\mathrm{X}|
of this conjecture,
respectively.

Proposition 5.8
.

For a subset
J\subseteq[d]
of cardinality at least
2
, let
\mathrm{X}_{J}\vdash[d]
be the partition whose only non-singleton block is
J
.
Then,

\log[\Delta_{J}]\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}_{J}}

is a nonzero element.
Furthermore,
\{\log[\Delta_{J}]\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}_{J}}\,:\,J\subseteq[d],\,|J|\geq 2\}
is a basis of simultaneous eigenvectors for
\Xi_{1}(\pi_{d})
.

Proof.

First, observe that any cyclic permutation on a set with more than one element has at least one excedance, and only one cyclic permutation attains this minimum. Namely, the only cyclic permutation in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
having one excedance is
(d\,d-1\,\dots\,2\,1)
.
Hence, a permutation
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
has at least as many excedances as non-singleton blocks in
\supp(\sigma)
.
It then follows from Theorem
5.1
that

\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\Xi_{1}(\pi_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if\penalty\ $\mathrm{X}\vdash[d]$ has exactly one non-singleton block},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Thus, the second statement follows from the first.

Since
\{\log[\Delta_{J}]\,:\,J\subseteq[d],\,\big|J\big|\geq 2\}
is a linear basis for
\Xi_{1}(\pi_{d})
,
it is enough to write
\log[\Delta_{J}]\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}_{J}}
as a non-trivial linear combination of these basis elements. Observe that if
F=(S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{k})
, then
[\Delta_{J}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}=[\Delta_{J\cap S_{i}}]
where
i
is the first index for which the intersection
J\cap S_{i}
is nonempty. Thus,

[\Delta_{J}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}=\begin{cases}[\Delta_{J}]&\text{if }\supp(F)\leq\mathrm{X}_{J},\\
[\text{a proper face of }\Delta_{J}]&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Using that the action of
\mathtt{H}_{F}
is an algebra morphism, we have
\log[\Delta_{J}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}=\log([\Delta_{J}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F})
.
Hence, the coefficient of
\log[\Delta_{J}]
in
\log[\Delta_{J}]\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}_{J}}
is

\dfrac{1}{\dim(\mathrm{X}_{J})!}\sum_{\supp(F)=\mathrm{X}_{J}}1=1.

The equality follows since for any flat
\mathrm{X}
of the braid arrangement,
\mathcal{A}_{d}^{\mathrm{X}}
has
\dim(\mathrm{X})!
chambers.
∎

Note that the element
\log[\Delta_{J}]\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}_{J}}
in the proposition is precisely the element
x_{\sigma}
for the unique permutation
\sigma
with
\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X}_{J}
and
\exc(\sigma)=1
.
Indeed,
t(\sigma)
consists of a increasing path whose nodes are the elements in
J
and isolated roots indexed by the elements in
[d]\setminus J
.

Proposition 5.9
.

For any
\mathrm{X}=\{S_{1},\dots,S_{k}\}\vdash[d]
, the space
\Xi_{d-k}(\pi_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}
is
1
-dimensional.
Moreover,

(5.8)

x_{\mathrm{X}}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Big(\prod_{j\neq\min(S_{i})}\log[\Delta_{\{\min(S_{i}),j\}}]\Big)

is a nonzero element in
\Xi_{d-k}(\pi_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}
.

Proof.

Observe that any cyclic permutation on a set with
s
elements has at most
s-1
excedances, and only one cyclic permutation attains this maximum.
Namely, the only cyclic permutation in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
having
d-1
excedances is
(1\,2\,\dots\,d-1\,d)
.
Hence, for any
\mathrm{X}=\{S_{1},\dots,S_{k}\}\vdash[d]
there is exactly one permutation with
\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X}
and
d-k
excedances.
Theorem
5.1
then implies that

\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\Xi_{d-k}(\pi_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}})=1.

It follows from Example
2.2
that the element
x_{\mathrm{X}}
is nonzero, and counting the number of factors in (
5.8
) shows that
x_{\mathrm{X}}\in\Xi_{d-k}(\pi_{d})
. Thus, we are only left to prove that
x_{\mathrm{X}}\in\Xi_{d-k}(\pi_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}
. That is, that
x_{\mathrm{X}}\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}=x_{\mathrm{X}}

Let
G\in\Sigma[\mathcal{A}_{d}]
with
\supp(G)>\mathrm{X}
.
Then, for some block
S_{i}\in\mathrm{X}
and some
a\in S_{i}
,
a
and
\min(S_{i})
are not in the same block of
\supp(G)
.
Hence
[\Delta_{\{\min(S_{i}),a\}}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{G}
is the class of a point, and
\log[\Delta_{\{\min(S_{i}),a\}}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{G}=\log[\{{\bf 0}\}]=0
.
Since the action of
\mathtt{H}_{G}
is an algebra morphism, we get that
x_{\mathrm{X}}\cdot\mathtt{H}_{G}=0
.
Therefore,

x_{\mathrm{X}}\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}=x_{\mathrm{X}}\cdot\Big(\dfrac{1}{\dim(\mathrm{X})!}\sum_{\supp(F)=\mathrm{X}}\mathtt{H}_{F}\Big)=\dfrac{1}{\dim(\mathrm{X})!}\sum_{\supp(F)=\mathrm{X}}x_{\mathrm{X}}\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}=x_{\mathrm{X}},

as we wanted to show.
∎

The element
x_{\mathrm{X}}
in the previous result is
x_{\sigma}
for the only permutation
\sigma
with
\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X}
and
\exc(\sigma)=d-|\mathrm{X}|
.
In this case, the forest
t(\sigma)
consists of a
k
trees with node sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{k}
, respectively.
Each tree has
\min(S_{i})
as a root and every other element in
S_{i}
as a child of the root.

6.
The module of type B generalized permutahedra

In this section, we study the algebra
\Pi(\pi^{B}_{d})
of
type B generalized permutahedra
and its structure as a module over the Tits algebra of the Coxeter arrangement of type B.

6.1.
The type B Coxeter arrangement

The
type B Coxeter arrangement

\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
consists of the hyperplanes

x_{i}=x_{j}
,
x_{i}=-x_{j}
for
1\leq i<j\leq d
and
x_{k}=0
for
1\leq k\leq d
.
Its central face is the trivial cone
\{{\bf 0}\}
.
The
Coxeter complex of type
B_{d}
, obtained by intersecting the arrangement with a sphere around the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, is shown below for
d=2
and
3
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}

Flats and faces of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
are in correspondence with signed set partitions and signed set compositions of
[\pm d]:=\{-d,-d+1,\dots,-1,1,\dots,d-1,d\}
, as originally introduced by Reiner
[
27
]
.

Let
I
be a finite set with an fixed point free involution
x\mapsto\overline{x}
.
For instance,
[\pm d]
with involution
\overline{x}=-x
.
A subset
S\subseteq I
is said to be
involution-exclusive
if
S\cap\overline{S}=\emptyset
, where
\overline{S}=\{\overline{x}\,:\,x\in S\}
.
In contrast,
S\subseteq I
is said to be
involution-inclusive
if
S=\overline{S}
.
Given an arbitrary subset
S\subseteq I
, we let
\pm S
be the involution-inclusive set
S\cup\overline{S}
.

A
signed set partition
of
I
is a weak set partition of the form
\mathrm{X}=\{S_{0},S_{1},\overline{S_{1}},\dots,S_{k},\overline{S_{k}}\}
, where
S_{0}
is involution-inclusive and allowed to be empty, and each
S_{i}
for
i\neq 0
is nonempty and involution-exclusive. We call
S_{0}
the
zero block
of
\mathrm{X}
.
We write
\mathrm{X}\vdash^{B}I
to denote that
\mathrm{X}
is a signed partition of
I
.
Given a signed partition
\mathrm{X}\vdash^{B}[\pm d]
, the corresponding flat of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
is the intersection of the hyperplanes
x_{i}=x_{j}
for each
i,j
in the same block of
\mathrm{X}
, where for
k\in[d]
, we let
x_{\overline{k}}
denote
-x_{k}
.
In particular, if
k\in[d]
is in the zero block of
\mathrm{X}
, the corresponding flat lies in the hyperplane
x_{k}=0
.
For instance, consider the following two examples for
d=7
:

\begin{array}[]{CCC}x_{1}=x_{3},\quad x_{2}=-x_{4}=x_{5},\quad x_{6}=x_{7}&\hskip 17.25105pt\longleftrightarrow&\{\emptyset,13,\bar{1}\bar{3},2\bar{4}5,\bar{2}4\bar{5},67,\bar{6}\bar{7}\},\\
x_{1}=x_{3}=0,\quad x_{2}=-x_{4}=x_{5},\quad x_{6}=x_{7}&\longleftrightarrow&\{1\bar{1}3\bar{3},2\bar{4}5,\bar{2}4\bar{5},67,\bar{6}\bar{7}\}.\\
\end{array}

The zero block in the first partition is empty since the corresponding flat is not contained in any coordinate hyperplane. We use
\mathrm{X}
to denote both a flat of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
and the corresponding signed partition of
[\pm d]
.
Observe that the number of nonzero blocks of
\mathrm{X}
is
2\dim(\mathrm{X})
.
The partial order relation of
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}]
becomes the ordering by refinement of signed partitions. For instance,

\{1\bar{1}3\bar{3},2\bar{4}5,\bar{2}4\bar{5},67,\bar{6}\bar{7}\}\leq\{\emptyset,13,\bar{1}\bar{3},2\bar{4}5,\bar{2}4\bar{5},67,\bar{6}\bar{7}\}\leq\{\emptyset,13,\bar{1}\bar{3},25,\bar{2}\bar{5},4,\bar{4},67,\bar{6}\bar{7}\}
.

If
S
is an involution-inclusive union of blocks of
\mathrm{X}\vdash^{B}I
, we let
\mathrm{X}|_{S}\vdash^{B}S
denote the corresponding signed partition.
If, on the other hand,
S
is an involution-exclusive union of blocks of
\mathrm{X}\vdash^{B}I
, we let
\mathrm{X}|_{S}\vdash S
denote the corresponding (type A) partition.

Let
\mathrm{X}=\{S_{0},S_{1},\overline{S_{1}},\dots,S_{k},\overline{S_{k}}\}
.
A choice of
\mathrm{Y}\geq\mathrm{X}
is equivalent to the choice of a signed partition
\mathrm{Y}|_{S_{0}}\vdash^{B}S_{0}
of the zero block and of (type A) partitions
\mathrm{Y}|_{S_{i}}\vdash S_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,k
.
Note that in this case,
\mathrm{Y}|_{\overline{S_{i}}}
is automatically determined by
\mathrm{Y}|_{S_{i}}
.

With
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{Y}
as above, the Möbius function of
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}]
is determined by

(6.1)

\mu(\perp,\mathrm{X})=(-1)^{k}(2k-1)!!\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mu(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})=\mu(\perp,\mathrm{Y}|_{S_{0}})\mu(\perp,\mathrm{Y}|_{S_{1}})\dots\mu(\perp,\mathrm{Y}|_{S_{k}}),

where
(2k-1)!!
is the double factorial
(2k-1)!!=(2k-1)(2k-3)\dots 1
,
and in each factor
\perp
denotes the minimum (signed) partition of (
S_{0}
)
S_{i}
.

A
signed composition
is an ordered signed set partition with the property that
S_{i}
precedes
S_{j}
if and only if
\overline{S_{j}}
precedes
\overline{S_{i}}
.
The following examples illustrate the identification between signed compositions of
\pm[d]
and faces of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
:

\begin{array}[]{CCC}x_{6}=x_{7}>-x_{2}=x_{4}=-x_{5}>x_{1}=x_{3}>0&\hskip 17.25105pt\longleftrightarrow&(67,\bar{2}4\bar{5},13,\emptyset,\bar{1}\bar{3},2\bar{4}5,\bar{6}\bar{7})\\
x_{6}=x_{7}>-x_{2}=x_{4}=-x_{5}>x_{1}=x_{3}=0&\longleftrightarrow&(67,\bar{2}4\bar{5},1\bar{1}3\bar{3},2\bar{4}5,\bar{6}\bar{7})\end{array}

Note that we can alternatively describe the first face with the inequalities
0>-x_{1}=-x_{3}>x_{2}=-x_{4}=x_{5}>-x_{6}=-x_{7}
.

6.2.
The hyperoctahedral group and the Type B Eulerian polynomial

The hyperoctahedral group
{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
is the group of bijections
\sigma:[\pm d]\rightarrow[\pm d]
satisfying
\sigma(\overline{i})=\overline{\sigma(i)}
for all
i\in[\pm d]
under composition. Elements in
{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
are called
signed permutations
.
The group
{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
acts on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by permutation and sign changes of coordinates:

\sigma(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d})=(x_{\sigma(1)},x_{\sigma(2)},\dots,x_{\sigma(d)}).

Recall that, for instance,
x_{\overline{1}}=-x_{1}
.
For a signed permutation
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
, we let
\supp(\sigma)
denote the subspace of points fixed by the action of
\sigma
; it is a flat of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
.
Under the identification above,
\supp(\sigma)
is the signed partition of
[\pm d]
obtained from the underlying the cycle decomposition of
\sigma
by merging all the blocks that contain an element
i
and its negative
\overline{i}
.
For example, if in cycle notation
\sigma=(1)(\bar{1})(2\bar{2})(34\bar{3}\bar{4})(5\bar{6})(\bar{5}6)
, then
\supp(\sigma)=\{2\bar{2}3\bar{3}4\bar{4},1,\bar{1},5\bar{6},\bar{5}6\}
.

Let
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{n}
.
The restriction
\sigma|_{S_{0}}
to the zero block
S_{0}\in\supp(\sigma)
is a signed permutation of
S_{0}
.
Its action on
\mathbb{R}^{|S_{0}|/2}
does not fix any nonzero vector, so
\supp(\sigma|_{S_{0}})=\perp
.
For a nonzero block
S\in\supp(\sigma)
,
\sigma|_{S}\in{\mathfrak{C}}(S)
is a cyclic permutation of the elements in
S
.
The restriction
\sigma|_{\pm S}
is again a signed permutation, and it is completely determined by either
\sigma|_{S}
or
\sigma|_{\overline{S}}
.

We present some statistics on signed permutations.
For
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
, let

\displaystyle\Des(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{i\in[d-1]\cup\{0\}\,:\,\sigma(i)>\sigma(i+1)\}

\displaystyle\des(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\big|\Des(\sigma)\big|

\displaystyle\Exc(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{i\in[d-1]\,:\,\sigma(i)>i\}

\displaystyle\exc(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\big|\Exc(\sigma)\big|

\displaystyle\Neg(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{i\in[d]\,:\,\sigma(i)<0\}

\displaystyle\fneg(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\big|\Neg(\sigma)\big|

\displaystyle\fexc(\sigma)

\displaystyle=2\exc(\sigma)+\fneg(\sigma),

where we set
\sigma(0)=0
.
Elements in the sets above are
descents
,
excedances
and
negations
of
\sigma
, respectively.
The last statistic is called the
flag-excedance
of a signed permutation.
We define one last statistic, the B-excedance of
\sigma
:

(6.2)

\exc_{B}(\sigma)=\lfloor\tfrac{\fexc(\sigma)+1}{2}\rfloor=\exc(\sigma)+\lfloor\tfrac{\fneg(\sigma)+1}{2}\rfloor.

Foata and Han
[
17
, Section 9]
show that descents and B-excedances are equidistributed.
That is,

B_{d,k}:=\big|\{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}\,:\,\des(\sigma)=k\}\big|=\big|\{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}\,:\,\exc_{B}(\sigma)=k\}\big|,

for all possible values of
k
.
The numbers
B_{d,k}
are the
Eulerian numbers of type B
(OEIS:
A060187
).
The
type B Eulerian polynomial

B_{d}(z)
is:

B_{d}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{d}B_{d,k}z^{k}=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}}z^{\exc_{B}(\sigma)}.

The exponential generating function of these polynomials is first due to Brenti
[
12
, Theorem 3.4]
.
We will be interested in the
type B exponential generating function
of these polynomials:

(6.3)

B(z,x)=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}B_{d}(z)\dfrac{x^{d}}{(2d)!!}=\dfrac{(1-z)e^{x(1-z)/2}}{1-ze^{x(1-z)}},

where
(2d)!!
is the double factorial
(2d)!!=(2d)(2d-2)\dots 2=2^{d}d!
.
Substituting
x
by
2x
one recovers Brenti’s original formula.

6.3.
The module of type B Generalized permutahedra

The type B permutahedron
\pi^{B}_{d}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the convex hull of the
{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
-orbit of the point
(1,2,\dots,d)
.
It is full-dimensional and a zonotope of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
.
We now consider the module
\Pi(\pi^{B}_{d})
.
The main result of this section is the following.

Theorem 6.1
.

For any flat
\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}]
and
r=0,1,\dots,d
,

\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}(\pi^{B}_{d}))=\big|\{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}\,:\,\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X},\ \exc_{B}(\sigma)=r\}\big|.
