Figure 6.1.
Type B permutahedron in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

As in type A, the relation between
\Pi(\pi^{B}_{d})
and statistics on
{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
is due to Brenti’s result showing that
h(\pi^{B}_{d},z)=B_{d}(z)
.
For a flat
\mathrm{X}=\{S_{0},S_{1},\overline{S_{1}},\dots,S_{k},\overline{S_{k}}\}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
,
the face
(\pi^{B}_{d})_{\mathrm{X}}
is a translate of
\pi^{B}_{|S_{0}|/2}\times\pi_{|S_{1}|}\times\dots\times\pi_{|S_{k}|}
, a product of lower-dimensional permutahedra of type A and B, where exactly one factor is of type B.
Thus,

(6.4)

h((\pi^{B}_{d})_{\mathrm{X}},z)=B_{|S_{0}|/2}(z)\cdot A_{|S_{1}|}(z)\cdot\ldots\cdot A_{|S_{k}|}(z).

The following result is the analogous in type B of Lemma
5.3
.

Lemma 6.2
.

For every
d\geq 1
,

(6.5)

\sum_{\mathrm{X}=\{S_{0},\dots,S_{k},\overline{S_{k}}\}\vdash^{B}[\pm d]}\mu(\perp,\mathrm{X})B_{|S_{0}|/2}(z)\cdot A_{|S_{1}|}(z)\cdot\ldots\cdot A_{|S_{k}|}(z)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}\\
\supp(\sigma)=\perp\end{subarray}}z^{\exc_{B}(\sigma)}.

In the same spirit as the proof of Lemma
5.3
, we will establish (
6.5
) by comparing the type B exponential generating function of both sides of the equality.
An important tool in this proof is the following analog of the Compositional Formula (Theorem
5.2
) for type B generating functions.

Proposition 6.3

(Type B Compositional Formula)
.

Let

f(x)=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}f_{d}\dfrac{x^{d}}{(2d)!!}\qquad g(x)=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}g_{d}\dfrac{x^{d}}{(2d)!!}\qquad a(x)=\sum_{d\geq 1}a_{d}\dfrac{x^{d}}{d!}.

If

h(x)=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}h_{d}\dfrac{x^{d}}{(2d)!!}\quad\text{where}\quad h_{d}=\sum_{\{S_{0},S_{1},\overline{S_{1}},\dots,S_{k},\overline{S_{k}}\}\vdash^{B}[\pm d]}f_{|S_{0}|/2}g_{k}a_{|S_{1}|}\dots a_{|S_{k}|},

then

h(x)=f(x)g(a(x)).

Proof.

Using the usual Compositional Formula, the coefficient of
\dfrac{x^{d}}{(2d)!!}
in
f(x)g(a(x))
is

\begin{array}[]{RL}&2^{d}\,d!\sum_{r=0}^{d}\dfrac{f_{r}}{2^{r}\,r!}\left(\dfrac{1}{(d-r)!}\sum_{\{K_{1},\dots,K_{k}\}\vdash[d-r]}\dfrac{g_{k}}{2^{k}}\,a_{|K_{1}|}\dots a_{|K_{k}|}\right)\\
=&\sum_{r=0}^{d}{d\choose r}\left(\sum_{\{K_{1},\dots,K_{k}\}\vdash[d-r]}2^{d-r-k}\,f_{r}\,g_{k}\,a_{|K_{1}|}\dots a_{|K_{k}|}\right)\\
=&\sum_{\{S_{0},S_{1},\overline{S_{1}},\dots,S_{k},\overline{S_{k}}\}\vdash^{B}[\pm d]}f_{|S_{0}|/2}\,g_{k}\,a_{|S_{1}|}\dots a_{|S_{k}|},\end{array}

this is precisely the coefficient of
\dfrac{x^{d}}{(2d)!!}
in
h(x)
.
To verify the last equality, note that choosing a type B partition
\{S_{0},S_{1},\overline{S_{1}},\dots,S_{k},\overline{S_{k}}\}\vdash^{B}[\pm d]
with
|S_{0}|=2r
is equivalent to:

(1)

choosing a subset
K_{0}\in{[d]\choose r}
and setting
S_{0}=\pm K_{0}
,

(2)

choosing a partition
\{K_{1},\dots,K_{k}\}
of
[d]\setminus K_{0}
, and

(3)

constructing blocks
\{S_{i},\overline{S_{i}}\}
from
K_{i}
as follows: for each
j\in K_{i}\setminus\{\max K_{i}\}
, choose whether
j
and
\max K_{i}
will be in the same or in
opposite
blocks.

There are precisely
2^{d-r-k}
possible choices in the last step.
∎

Taking
g_{d}=1
in the Type B Compositional Formula we deduce the following.

Corollary 6.4

(Type B Exponential Formula)
.

Let
f(x)
and
a(x)
be as before.
If

h(x)=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}h_{d}\dfrac{x^{d}}{(2d)!!}\qquad\text{where}\qquad h_{d}=\sum_{\mathrm{X}\vdash^{B}[\pm d]}f_{|S_{0}|/2}a_{|S_{1}|}\dots a_{|S_{k}|},

then

h(x)=f(x)\,\exp(a(x)/2).

In the proof of Theorem
5.1
, we used that for (type A) permutations
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
,
\exc(\sigma)
equals the sum of
\exc(\sigma|_{S})
as
S
runs through the blocks of
\supp(\sigma)
.
For signed permutations, one easily checks this also holds for the statistics
\exc
and
\fneg
.
However, it is not obvious at all that the same is true for
\exc_{B}
, since its definition uses the floor function.

Consider the order
\prec
of the elements of any involution-exclusive subset
S\subseteq[\pm d]
defined by:

(6.6)

i\prec j\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}0<i<j,\text{ or}\\
i<0<j,\text{ or}\\
j<i<0.\end{cases}

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
and
\supp(\sigma)=\{S_{0},S_{1},\overline{S_{1}},\dots,S_{k},\overline{S_{k}}\}
.
Then,

\exc_{B}(\sigma)=\exc_{B}(\sigma|_{S_{0}})+\exc_{\prec}(\sigma|_{S_{1}})+\dots+\exc_{\prec}(\sigma|_{S_{k}}),

where
\exc_{\prec}(\sigma|_{S_{i}})
is the number of usual
(
type A
)
excedances of
\sigma|_{S_{i}}
with respect to the order
\prec
.

Proof.

For
i\geq 1
, write
\sigma|_{S_{i}}=(j_{1}j_{2}\dots j_{\ell})
in cycle notation.
Since
\sigma|_{\overline{S_{i}}}=(\overline{j_{1}}\,\overline{j_{2}}\dots\overline{j_{\ell}})
,
negations of
\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}}
are in correspondence with changes of sign in the sequence

j_{1}\mapsto j_{2}\mapsto\dots\mapsto j_{\ell}\mapsto j_{1}.

It follows that

\fneg(\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}})=2\cdot\big|\{j\in S_{i}\,:\,j<0<\sigma(j)\}\big|

is an even number.

Observe that according to the three cases of definition (
6.6
), a
\prec
-excedance of
\sigma|_{S_{i}}
corresponds to either

\begin{cases}\text{an excedance of }\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}}\text{ occurring in }S_{i},\text{ or}\\
\text{a negation of }\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}}\text{ occurring in }\overline{S_{i}},\text{ or}\\
\text{an excedance of }\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}}\text{ occurring in }\overline{S_{i}},\end{cases}

respectively.
Since exactly half of the negations of
\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}}
occur in
\overline{S_{i}}
, we deduce that

\exc_{\prec}(\sigma|_{S_{i}})=\exc(\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}})+\tfrac{\fneg(\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}})}{2}.

Thus, in view of (
6.2
) and using that
\tfrac{\fneg(\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}})}{2}
is always an integer,

\begin{array}[]{RL}\exc_{B}(\sigma)=&\exc(\sigma)+\lfloor\tfrac{\fneg(\sigma)+1}{2}\rfloor\\
=&\exc(\sigma|_{S_{0}})+\sum_{i}\exc(\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}})+\lfloor\tfrac{\fneg(\sigma|_{S_{0}})+\sum_{i}\fneg(\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}})+1}{2}\rfloor\\
=&\exc(\sigma|_{S_{0}})+\sum_{i}\exc(\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}})+\lfloor\tfrac{\fneg(\sigma|_{S_{0}})+1}{2}\rfloor+\sum_{i}\tfrac{\fneg(\sigma|_{\pm S_{i}})}{2}\\
=&\exc_{B}(\sigma|_{S_{0}})+\exc_{\prec}(\sigma|_{S_{1}})+\dots+\exc_{\prec}(\sigma|_{S_{k}}),\end{array}

as we wanted to show.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

Recall that
\mu(\perp,\mathrm{X})=(-1)^{k}(2k-1)!!
, where
|\mathrm{X}|=2k+1
.
Observe that

1+\sum_{d\geq 1}(-1)^{d}(2d-1)!!\dfrac{x^{d}}{(2d)!!}=\sum_{d\geq 0}{-1/2\choose d}x^{d}=(1+x)^{-1/2}.

Using the Type B Compositional formula, we conclude that the type B exponential generating function of the LHS of (
6.5
) is

B(z,x)(1+(A(z,x)-1))^{-1/2}-1=\dfrac{B(z,x)}{\sqrt{A(z,x)}}-1,

where
A(z,x)
and
B(z,x)
are the generating functions in (
5.2
) and (
6.3
), respectively.
On the other hand, Proposition
6.5
shows that for each partition
\mathrm{X}=\{S_{0},S_{1},\overline{S_{1}},\dots,S_{k},\overline{S_{k}}\}\vdash^{B}[\pm d]
,

(6.7)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}\\
\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X}\end{subarray}}z^{\exc_{B}(\sigma)}=\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{0}\in{\mathfrak{B}}(S_{0})\\
\supp(\sigma_{0})=\perp\end{subarray}}z^{\exc_{B}(\sigma_{0})}\Big)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Big(\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{C}}(|S_{i}|)}z^{\exc(\sigma)}\Big).

In the proof of Lemma
5.3
, we showed that the (usual) exponential generating function of the terms in the product is
\log(A(z,x))
.
An application of the type B Exponential Formula and (
6.7
) yields

B(z,x)=\bigg(1+\sum_{d\geq 1}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}\\
\supp(\sigma)=\perp\end{subarray}}z^{\exc_{B}(\sigma)}\Big)\dfrac{x^{d}}{(2d)!!}\bigg)\,\exp\Big(\dfrac{\log(A(z,x))}{2}\Big)

Dividing both sides by
\exp\big(\tfrac{\log(A(z,x))}{2}\big)=\sqrt{A(z,x)}
and subtracting
1
yields the result.
∎

We are now ready to complete the proof of the main theorem in this section.
The steps of the proof mirror those of the type A result.

Proof of
Theorem
6.1
.

We will use formula (
4.3
) to compute the values
\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}(\pi^{B}_{d}))
.
Recall that the formula reads

\sum_{r}\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}(\pi^{B}_{d}))z^{r}\sum_{\mathrm{Y}\geq\mathrm{X}}\mu(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})h((\pi^{B}_{d})_{\mathrm{Y}},z).

Using formulas (
6.1
) and (
6.4
), we see that for a flat
\mathrm{X}=\{S_{0},\dots,S_{k},\overline{S_{k}}\}
this expression equals the following product

\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{Y}\vdash^{B}S_{0}\\
\mathrm{Y}=\{T_{0},\dots,T_{\ell},\overline{T_{\ell}}\}\end{subarray}}\mu(\perp,\mathrm{Y})B_{|T_{0}|/2}A_{|T_{1}|}\dots A_{|T_{\ell}|}\bigg)\cdot\\
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{Y}_{i}\vdash S_{i}\\
\mathrm{Y}_{i}=\{T^{i}_{1},\dots,T^{i}_{\ell}\}\end{subarray}}\mu(\perp,\mathrm{Y}_{i})A_{|T^{i}_{1}|}(z)\cdot\ldots\cdot A_{|T^{i}_{\ell}|}(z)\bigg).

Using Lemmas
5.3
and
6.2
in each factor, we deduce

\sum_{r}\eta_{\mathrm{X}}(\Xi_{r}(\pi^{B}_{d}))z^{r}=\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}(S_{0})\\
\supp(\sigma)=\perp\end{subarray}}z^{\exc_{B}(\sigma)}\bigg)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Big(\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{C}}(|S_{i}|)}z^{\exc(\sigma)}\Big)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}\\
\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X}\end{subarray}}z^{\exc_{B}(\sigma)},

where the last equality is (
6.7
).
Finally, taking the coefficient of
z^{r}
on both sides yields the result.
∎

Adding over all flats with the same dimension in Theorem
6.1
, we conclude the following.

Corollary 6.6
.

Let
w\in\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}]
be a characteristic element of non-critical parameter
t
and
\lambda>0
.
The dimension of the simultaneous eigenspace for
w
and
\delta_{\lambda}
with eigenvalues
t^{k}
and
\lambda^{r}
is

\big|\{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}\,:\,\dim(\supp(\sigma))=k,\ \exc_{B}(\sigma)=r\}\big|.

As in the type A case, we can modify the proof of the previous Lemma to obtain the generating function for the bivariate polynomials
\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}}t^{\dim(\supp(\sigma))}z^{\exc_{B}(\sigma)}
.
To the best of our knowledge, this is a new result.

Proposition 6.7
.

The following identity holds

1+\sum_{d\geq 1}\Big(\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}}t^{\dim(\supp(\sigma))}z^{\exc_{B}(\sigma)}\Big)\dfrac{x^{n}}{(2n)!!}=\dfrac{(1-z)e^{x(1-z)/2}}{1-ze^{x(1-z)}}\,\left(\dfrac{z-1}{z-e^{x(z-1)}}\right)^{\tfrac{t-1}{2}}.

Proof.

We can slightly modify (
6.7
) to obtain

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}\\
\supp(\sigma)=\mathrm{X}\end{subarray}}t^{\dim(\mathrm{X})}z^{\exc_{B}(\sigma)}=\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}(S_{0})\\
\supp(\sigma)=\perp\end{subarray}}z^{\exc_{B}(\sigma_{0})}\Big)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Big(t\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{C}}(|S_{i}|)}z^{\exc(\sigma)}\Big).

Using the (type B) generating functions of the factors deduced in the proofs of Lemmas
5.3
and
6.2
, and the type B compositional formula, we deduce

1+\sum_{d\geq 1}\Big(\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}}t^{\dim(\supp(\sigma))}z^{\exc_{B}(\sigma)}\Big)\dfrac{x^{n}}{(2n)!!}=\dfrac{B(z,x)}{\sqrt{A(z,x)}}\exp\Big(\dfrac{t\log(A(z,x))}{2}\Big),

which equals
B(z,x)A(z,x)^{\tfrac{t-1}{2}}
.
Substituting the expressions for
A(z,x)
and
B(z,x)
in (
5.2
) and (
6.3
) yields the result.
∎

Specializing
t:=0
and
x:=2x
gives an alternative expression for the exponential generating function of the OEIS sequence
A156919
.
In our context, these coefficients count the number of signed permutations whose action on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
has no nonzero fixed point weighted by the statistic
\exc_{B}
.

6.4.
Two bases basis for type B generalized permutahedra

Faces of the standard simplex
\Delta_{[d]}
correspond to linearly independent rays of the cone of generalized permutahedra in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In the language of the present work, this means that
\{\log[\Delta_{S}]\,:\,S\subseteq[d],\,|S|\geq 2\}
forms a linear basis of
\Xi_{1}(\pi_{d})
and that
\log[\Delta_{S}]
is not the sum of the log-classes of other generalized permutahedra (other than trivial dilations of itself).
The goal of this section is to establish an analogous result for the type B case.

The standard simplex coincides with the
weight polytope

P_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}}(\lambda_{1})
in the sense of Ardila, Castillo, Eur, and Postnikov
[
7
]
, where
\lambda_{1}
is the fundamental weight
(1,0,\dots,0)
of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
. In this manner, the cross-polytope
P_{{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}}(\lambda_{1})=\Conv\{\pm e_{i}\,:\,i\in[d]\}
is the type B analog of the standard simplex.
However, in the same paper the authors point out that the faces of the cross-polytope span a space of roughly half the desired dimension.
This is intuitively clear once we notice that the collection of faces of
P_{{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}}(\lambda_{1})
entirely contained in one orthant span the same space in
\Xi_{1}(\pi^{B}_{d})
as the faces contained in the opposite orthant.
The following result shows that it is not possible to find a single type B generalized permutahedron whose faces generate
\Xi_{1}(\pi^{B}_{d})
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}=\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha}
be a collection of type B generalized permutahedra such that
\{\log[{\mathfrak{p}}_{\alpha}]\}_{\alpha}
spans
\Xi_{1}(\pi^{B}_{d})
. Then,
{\mathcal{P}}
contains at least
2^{d-1}
full dimensional polytopes.

Proof.

Let
\{\mathtt{E}_{\mathrm{X}}\}_{\mathrm{X}}\subseteq\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}]
be an Eulerian family of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
.
The result follows from the following two facts, which we justify below.

(1)

The projection of
\log[{\mathfrak{p}}]
to the subspace
\Xi_{1}(\pi^{B}_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\perp}
is zero unless
{\mathfrak{p}}
is full-dimensional.

(2)

\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\Xi_{1}(\pi^{B}_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\perp})=2^{d-1}
.

(1)

Let
{\mathfrak{p}}
be a type B generalized permutahedron that is not full-dimensional.
Then we can choose a face
F\neq O
of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
such that
{\mathfrak{p}}_{F}={\mathfrak{p}}
, for instance any maximal face in
N({\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{p}})
.
It follows from
[
1
, Lemma 11.12]
that
\mathtt{H}_{F}\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\perp}=0
.
Therefore,

\log[{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\perp}=\log([{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\perp}=\log[{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot(\mathtt{H}_{F}\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\perp})=0.

(2)

Recall that by definition,
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\Xi_{1}(\pi^{B}_{d})\cdot\mathtt{E}_{\perp})=\eta_{\perp}(\Xi_{1}(\pi^{B}_{d}))
.
Using Theorem
6.1
, this is the number of signed permutations
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
with
\supp(\sigma)=\perp
and
\exc_{B}(\sigma)=1
.
A signed permutation
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
with
\supp(\sigma)=\perp
is the product of cycles on involution-inclusive subsets
S\subseteq[\pm d]
.
Moreover, each such cycle adds at least
1
to the number of negations of
\sigma
.
Thus, a signed permutation with
\supp(\sigma)=\perp
and
\exc_{B}(\sigma)=1
must be the product of either
1
or
2
cycles and have no excedances.
Such cycles are necessarily of the form
(d\,d-1\,\dots 1\overline{d}\,\overline{d-1}\dots\overline{1})
.
Thus, the permutations counted by
\eta_{\perp}(\Xi_{1}(\pi^{B}_{d}))
are in correspondence with
unordered
pairs
\{S,[\pm d]\setminus S\}
of involution-inclusive subsets of
[\pm d]
, and there are precisely
2^{d-1}
many of them.

Alternatively, one can manipulate the generating function in Proposition
6.7
(
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\big|_{t,z=0}
) to deduce that the coefficient of
t^{0}z^{1}x^{d}
is
2^{d-1}
.
∎

In
[
24
]
, Padrol, Pilaud, and Ritter construct a family of type B generalized permutahedra called
shard polytopes
.
They show that any type B generalized permutahedron can be written uniquely as a signed Minkowski sum of these polytopes (up to translation).
Already in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, there are
14
full-dimensional shard polytopes.
We proceed to construct a family of generators that achieves the minimum imposed by the previous proposition.

For a nonempty involution-exclusive subset
S\subseteq[\pm d]
, define the simplices

\Delta_{S}=\Conv\{e_{i}\,\mid\,i\in S\}\qquad\qquad\Delta^{0}_{S}=\Conv(\{{\bf 0}\}\cup\{e_{i}\,\,:\,\,i\in S\}),

where for
i\in[d]
,
e_{\overline{i}}=-e_{i}
.
Observe that the only full-dimensional simplices in this collection are
\Delta^{0}_{S}
with
|S|=d
.
We say that an involution-exclusive subset
S\subseteq[\pm d]
is
special
if in addition
\min\{|i|\,:\,i\in S\}\in S
.
From now on, we will only consider simplices
\Delta_{S}
and
\Delta^{0}_{S}
for special sets
S
.

Theorem 6.9
.

Every type B generalized permutahedron can be written uniquely as a signed Minkowski sum of the simplices
\Delta_{S}
and
\Delta^{0}_{S}
with
S
as above.

Figure 6.2.
The type B generalized permutahedra that are
signed Minkowski generators
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we only show the
4=2^{3-1}
full-dimensional generators.

Proof.

Observe that there are exactly
d
zero-dimensional polytopes in this collection:
\Delta_{S}
with
|S|=1
.
Since they minimally generate all translations in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
h_{1}(\pi^{B}_{d})=3^{d}-d-1
, the statement is equivalent to showing that the collection

(6.8)

\{\log[\Delta_{S}]\,:\,S\text{ special},\,|S|\geq 2\}\cup\{\log[\Delta^{0}_{S}]\,:\,S\text{ special}\}

is linearly independent in
\Xi_{1}(\pi^{B}_{d})
.
To prove this, we will use the following linear map, whose existence is guaranteed by
[
21
, Theorem 5]
:

\begin{array}[]{RCL}\Psi_{1}:\Xi_{1}(\pi^{B}_{d})&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}\Sigma^{d-1}[\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}]\\
\log[{\mathfrak{p}}]&\longmapsto&\sum_{F}\vol_{1}({\mathfrak{p}}_{F})\mathtt{H}_{F}.\end{array}

Here,
\Sigma^{d-1}[\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}]
denotes the collection of
(d-1)
-dimensional faces of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
and
\vol_{1}
denotes the
1
-dimensional normalized volume.
See Section
8
for more details.

The
edges
(
1
-dimensional faces) of
\Delta_{S}
are of the form
\Delta_{ab}:=\Delta_{\{a,b\}}
for distinct
a,b\in S
.
On the other hand, the edges of
\Delta^{0}_{S}
are of the form
\Delta_{ab}
for distinct
a,b\in S
or of the form
\Delta^{0}_{a}:=\Delta^{0}_{\{a\}}
for
a\in S
.
The normalized volume of all such edges is
1
.
Thus,
\log[\Delta_{S}]
(resp.
\log[\Delta^{0}_{S}]
) is mapped to the sum of
\mathtt{H}_{F}
as
F
ranges over the faces of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
that are maximal inside the normal cone of each edge of
\Delta_{S}
(resp.
\Delta^{0}_{S}
).

The following is an explicit description of the normal cones for the edges of
\Delta_{S}
and
\Delta^{0}_{S}
. Recall that if
j\in[d]
, then
x_{\overline{j}}
denotes
-x_{j}
.

(6.9)

\begin{array}[]{RL}N(\Delta_{ab},\Delta_{S})=&\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,x_{a}=x_{b}>x_{s}\text{ for all }s\in S\setminus\{a,b\}\}\\
N(\Delta_{ab},\Delta^{0}_{S})=&\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,x_{a}=x_{b}>0,\,x_{a}=x_{b}>x_{s}\text{ for all }s\in S\setminus\{a,b\}\}\\
N(\Delta^{0}_{a},\Delta^{0}_{S})=&\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,x_{a}=0>x_{s}\text{ for all }s\in S\setminus\{a\}\}\end{array}

Suppose

\varphi=\sum\alpha_{S}\log[\Delta_{S}]+\sum\beta_{S}\log[\Delta^{0}_{S}]=0.

By studying the coefficient of different faces of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
in
\Psi_{1}(\varphi)
, we will conclude that all the coefficients
\alpha_{S}
and
\beta_{S}
are necessarily
0
, and therefore the collection (
6.8
) is linearly independent.

We will start with the coefficients
\alpha
.
Fix
k\in[d-1]
, we prove by induction on
|S|
that
\alpha_{S}=0
for all
S
such that
k=\min\{|i|\,:\,i\in S\}
.
The base case is
|S|=2
.
Let
S=\{k,j\}
with
j\in\pm[k+1,d]
and consider the following face of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}

x_{1}>x_{2}>\dots>x_{k-1}>x_{\overline{k}}=x_{\overline{j}}>x_{k+1}>\dots>\widehat{x_{|j|}}>\dots>x_{d}>0,

or equivalently,

0>x_{\overline{d}}>\dots>\widehat{x_{\overline{|j|}}}>\dots>x_{\overline{k+1}}>x_{k}=x_{j}>x_{\overline{k-1}}>\dots>x_{\overline{1}}.

The hat over
x_{|j|}
denotes that the corresponding inequality is missing (since we already imposed
x_{|j|}=\pm x_{k}
).
By the description of the normal cones in (
6.9
), we observe that this face only appears in a normal cone of either:

•

\Delta_{S^{\prime}}
or
\Delta^{0}_{S^{\prime}}
with
\overline{k},\overline{j}\in S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime}\setminus\{\overline{k},\overline{j}\}\subseteq\pm[k+1,d]\cup\overline{[k-1]}
, or

•

\Delta_{S^{\prime}}
with
k,j\in S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime}\setminus\{k,j\}\subseteq\overline{[k-1]}
.

Since we are only considering special sets
S^{\prime}
(that is, such that
\min\{|i|\,:\,i\in S^{\prime}\}\in S^{\prime}
) and
k>0
, the only possibility is
S^{\prime}=\{k,j\}=S
.
Thus, the coefficient of this face in
\Psi_{1}(\varphi)
is
\alpha_{\{k,j\}}=0
.

Now suppose
S=\{k,j_{1},\dots,j_{r}\}
with
j_{1},\dots,j_{r}\in\pm[k+1,d]
.
If we proceed with the previous analysis for the face

0>x_{\overline{d}}>\dots>\widehat{x_{\overline{|j_{i}|}}}>\dots>x_{\overline{k+1}}>x_{k}=x_{j_{1}}>x_{j_{2}}>\dots>x_{j_{r}}>x_{\overline{k-1}}>\dots>x_{\overline{1}},

we conclude that it appears in a normal cone of
\Delta_{S^{\prime}}
if and only if
\{k,j_{1}\}\subseteq S^{\prime}\subseteq S
.
Thus, the coefficient of this face in
\Psi_{1}(\varphi)
is
\sum_{\{k,j_{1}\}\subseteq S^{\prime}\subseteq S}\alpha_{S^{\prime}}=0
.
By induction we have
\alpha_{S^{\prime}}=0
for all
\{k,j_{1}\}\subseteq S^{\prime}\subsetneq S
, and so we conclude
\alpha_{S}=0
.

We proceed now with the coefficients
\beta
.
Fix
k\in[d-1]
and
j\in\pm[k+1,d]
, and let
{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}(k,j)
be the collection of special subsets
S
such that
k,j\in S
and
k=\min\{|i|\,:\,i\in S\}
.
We first prove that
\beta_{S}=0
for any
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
.
Let
S=\{k,j,s_{1},\dots,s_{r}\}\in{\mathcal{S}}
and let
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{r^{\prime}}\}=[k+1,d]\setminus\{|j|,|s_{1}|,\dots,|s_{r}|\}
.
Consider the following
(d-1)
-dimensional face of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
:

x_{1}>\dots>x_{k-1}>x_{\overline{s_{1}}}>\dots>x_{\overline{s_{r}}}>x_{k}=x_{j}>x_{t_{1}}>\dots>x_{t_{r^{\prime}}}>0.

Its coefficient in
\Psi_{1}(\varphi)
is

y_{S}=\sum_{S^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}},\,S^{\prime}\cap\overline{S}=\emptyset}\beta_{S^{\prime}}=0,

We claim that these relations, one for each
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
, imply that
\beta_{S}=0
for all
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
.
This follows since for any
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
,

\sum_{T\in{\mathcal{S}},\,T\cap\overline{S}=\emptyset}(-1)^{|T|}y_{\overline{T}}=\sum_{T\in{\mathcal{S}},\,T\cap\overline{S}=\emptyset}\bigg(\sum_{S^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}},\,S^{\prime}\cap T=\emptyset}(-1)^{|T|}\beta_{S^{\prime}}\bigg)\\
=\sum_{S^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in{\mathcal{S}},\,T\cap\overline{S}=\emptyset\\
T\cap S^{\prime}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|T|}\bigg)\beta_{S^{\prime}}=\pm\beta_{S}.

The last equality follows from the next observations:

•

If
S^{\prime}=S=\{k,j,s_{1},\dots,s_{r}\}
, then the sum is over all
T\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
\{k,j\}\subseteq T\subseteq\{k,j\}\cup\pm\big([k+1,d]\setminus\{j,s_{1},\dots,s_{r}\}\big)
.
Then, this sum is of the form

\sum_{v\in\{-1,0,1\}^{\ell}}(-1)^{|v|}

where
\ell=d-k-r-1\geq 0
and
|v|
is the number of nonzero entries of
v
.
This sum is
(-1+1-1)^{\ell}=(-1)^{\ell}
.

•

If
S^{\prime}\neq S
and there is an element
i\in S\setminus S^{\prime}
, then the subsets in the sum come in pairs
T,T^{\prime}
where
i\in T
and
T^{\prime}=T\setminus\{i\}
.
Since
(-1)^{|T|}+(-1)^{|T^{\prime}|}=0
, this shows that the whole sum is
0
in this case.

•

If
S^{\prime}\neq S
and
S\subsetneq S^{\prime}
, we can chose
i\in S^{\prime}\setminus S
and repeat the previous argument with pairs
T,T^{\prime}
where
\overline{i}\in T
and
T^{\prime}=T\setminus\{\overline{i}\}
.

Since each special subset
S
with
|S|\geq 2
is in
{\mathcal{S}}(k,j)
for some
k,j
, we are only left to prove that
\beta_{\{k\}}=0
for all
k\in[d]
.
For fixed
k\in[d]
, consider the following face of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{d}
:

x_{k}=0>x_{d}>x_{d-1}>\dots>\widehat{x_{k}}>\dots>x_{1}.

The coefficient of this face in
\Psi_{1}(\varphi)
is
\sum_{\{k\}\subseteq S\subseteq[d]}\beta_{S}=0
.
Since we have shown that
\beta_{S}=0
for any
S
with
|S|\geq 2
, we conclude that
\beta_{\{k\}}=0
.
∎

Remark 6.10
.

Observe that the generating collection
\{\Delta_{S}\,:\,S\subseteq[d]\}
for generalized permutahedra is invariant under the action of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{d}
.
In contrast, the collection of generators for type B generalized permutahedra presented in the previous theorem fails to be invariant under the action of
{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
. This is not by accident. Already in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we see that any collection of type B generalized permutahedra that contains a triangle
{\mathfrak{p}}
(full-dimensional simplex) and that is invariant under the action of
{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
, will contain the rotations of
{\mathfrak{p}}
by
90^{\circ}
,
180^{\circ}
, and
270^{\circ}
, all of which are necessarily different. Thus, such a collection will not attain the minimum number of full-dimensional polytopes required by Proposition
6.8
.
In the following theorem, we present a different collection of generators for type B generalized permutahedra that is invariant under the action of
{\mathfrak{B}}_{d}
.

Theorem 6.11
.

Every type B generalized permutahedron can be written uniquely as a signed Minkowski sum of the simplices
\{\Delta^{0}_{S}\,:\,S\subseteq[\pm d]

involution-exclusive
\}
.

Proof.

First observe that for every
i\in[d]
,
\Delta^{0}_{\overline{i}}
is a translation of
\Delta^{0}_{i}
; explicitly
\Delta^{0}_{\overline{i}}+\Delta_{i}=\Delta^{0}_{i}
.
Then, we could replace the pair
\Delta^{0}_{i},\Delta^{0}_{\overline{i}}
in the statement with the pair
\Delta^{0}_{i},\Delta_{i}
.
Since
\{\Delta_{i}\,:\,i\in[d]\}
minimally generates the translations in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the statement is equivalent to showing that the collection

(6.10)

\{\log[\Delta^{0}_{S}]\,:\,|S|\geq 2\}\cup\{\log[\Delta^{0}_{i}]\,:\,i\in[d]\}

is a basis of
\Xi_{1}(\pi^{B}_{d})
.
By comparing the number of elements, we see that it is enough to write the elements in the basis (
6.8
) as a linear combination of the elements in the proposed basis above.
Since the proposed basis already contains all the elements of the form
\log[\Delta^{0}_{S}]
, we are only left to write
\log[\Delta_{S}]
for
|S|\geq 2
as a linear combination of the proposed basis. We do this by induction on
|S|
.

We use
\lambda=-1
and
r=1
in Theorem
2.6
to conclude that for any polytope
{\mathfrak{p}}
:

\log[-{\mathfrak{p}}]=-\sum_{{\mathfrak{q}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}}(-1)^{\dim({\mathfrak{q}})}\log[{\mathfrak{q}}].

Applying this identity to
{\mathfrak{p}}=\Delta^{0}_{S}=-\Delta^{0}_{\overline{S}}
yields

(6.11)

\log[\Delta_{S}]=(-1)^{|S|}\log[\Delta^{0}_{\overline{S}}]+\sum_{T\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S\setminus T|}\log[\Delta^{0}_{T}]-\sum_{T\subsetneq S}(-1)^{|S\setminus T|}\log[\Delta_{T}].

If
S=\{i,j\}
, this is

\log[\Delta_{S}]=\log[\Delta^{0}_{\overline{S}}]+\log[\Delta^{0}_{S}]-\log[\Delta^{0}_{i}]-\log[\Delta^{0}_{j}].

Observe that all of the terms in the right are in the proposed basis, so we have completed the base case of the induction.
If
|S|>2
, we use (
6.11
) and observe that, by induction hypothesis, all the terms in the right can be written as a linear combination of the proposed basis.
∎

7.
Hopf monoid structure

Combinatorial species were originally introduced by Joyal
[
20
]
as a tool for studying generating power series from a combinatorial perspective.
A comprehensive introduction to the theory of species can be found in the work by Bergeron, Labelle, and Leroux
[
9
]
.
The category of species possesses more than one monoidal structure.
Of central interest for the present work are the
Cauchy
and
Hadamard
product.
Aguiar and Mahajan
[
3
,
4
]
have explored these structures extensively, and have exploited this rich algebraic structure to obtain outstanding combinatorial results.
The first of these structures leads to the definition of Hopf monoids in species, a very active topic of research in recent years.

Aguiar and Ardila introduced the Hopf monoid of generalized permutahedra
{\sf GP}
in
[
1
]
.
It contains many other interesting combinatorial Hopf monoids as submonoids.
In this section we show that the valuation (
2.1
) and translation invariance (
2.2
) properties define a Hopf monoid quotient of
{\sf GP}
.

7.1.
Hopf monoids in a nutshell

Let
{\sf set^{\times}}
denote the category of finite sets with bijections as morphisms, and
{\sf Vec}
the category of vector spaces and linear maps.
The category of species
{\sf Sp}
is the functor category
[{\sf set^{\times}},{\sf Vec}]
.
It is a symmetric monoidal category under the
Cauchy product
.
The Cauchy product of two species
{\mathsf{p}}
and
{\mathsf{q}}
is

({\mathsf{p}}\bm{\cdot}{\mathsf{q}})[I]=\bigoplus_{I=S\sqcup T}{\mathsf{p}}[S]\otimes{\mathsf{q}}[T].

We say a species
{\mathsf{h}}
is a
Hopf monoid
if it is a bimonoid with an
antipode
in this monoidal category.

Let us make these definitions explicit.
A
species

{\mathsf{p}}
consists of the following data:

i.

For each finite set
I
, a vector space
{\mathsf{p}}[I]
.

ii.

For each bijection
\sigma:I\rightarrow J
, a linear isomorphism
{\mathsf{p}}[\sigma]:{\mathsf{p}}[I]\rightarrow{\mathsf{p}}[J]
.
These linear maps satisfy

{\mathsf{p}}[\sigma\circ\tau]={\mathsf{p}}[\sigma]\circ{\mathsf{p}}[\tau]\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\mathsf{p}}[\Id]=\Id.

A morphism of species
f:{\mathsf{p}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{q}}
is a collection of linear maps

f_{I}:{\mathsf{p}}[I]\rightarrow{\mathsf{q}}[I],

one for each finite set
I
, that commute with bijections.
That is,
f_{J}\circ{\mathsf{p}}[\sigma]={\mathsf{q}}[\sigma]\circ f_{I}

for any bijection
\sigma:I\rightarrow J
.

A
Hopf monoid
is a species
{\mathsf{h}}
together a collection of product, coproduct and antipode maps

\begin{array}[]{rccccrccccrccc}\mu_{S,T}:&{\mathsf{h}}[S]\otimes{\mathsf{h}}[T]&\rightarrow&{\mathsf{h}}[I]&&\Delta_{S,T}:&{\mathsf{h}}[I]&\rightarrow&{\mathsf{h}}[S]\otimes{\mathsf{h}}[T]&&\apode_{I}:&{\mathsf{h}}[I]&\rightarrow&{\mathsf{h}}[I]\\
&x\otimes y&\mapsto&x\bm{\cdot}y&&&z&\mapsto&\sum z|_{S}\otimes z/_{S}&&&z&\mapsto&\apode_{I}(z)\end{array}

for all finite sets
I
and decompositions
I=S\sqcup T
.
This morphisms satisfy certain naturality, (co)unitality, (co)associativity and compatibility axioms.
See
[
4
, Section 2]
and
[
1
]
for more details.

A Hopf monoid
{\mathsf{h}}
is
commutative
if
x\bm{\cdot}y=y\bm{\cdot}x
for all decompositions
I=S\sqcup T
and structures
x\in{\mathsf{h}}[S]
,
y\in{\mathsf{h}}[T]
.
If
{\mathsf{h}}
is a commutative Hopf monoid, then each space
{\mathsf{h}}[[d]]
has the structure of a right
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}_{d}]
-module, as follows.
Let
F\in\Sigma[\mathcal{A}_{d}]
be the face associated to a composition
(S_{1},\dots,S_{k})
of
[d]
. The associativity and coassociativity axioms imply that there are well defined maps

\mu_{F}:{\mathsf{h}}[S_{1}]\otimes\dots\otimes{\mathsf{h}}[S_{k}]\rightarrow{\mathsf{h}}[[d]]\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta_{F}:{\mathsf{h}}[[d]]\rightarrow{\mathsf{h}}[S_{1}]\otimes\dots\otimes{\mathsf{h}}[S_{k}],

obtained by iterating the product and coproduct maps in any meaningful way.
Then,
{\mathsf{h}}[[d]]
is a right
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}_{d}]
-module with the following structure:

x\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}=\mu_{F}\circ\Delta_{F}(x).

We will see that the module structure on generalized permutahedra in Section
5
arises in this manner.

7.2.
Generalized permutahedra and the McMullen (co)ideal

As a species,
{\sf GP}[I]
is the vector space with basis

\GP[I]=\{{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{I}\,:\,{\mathfrak{p}}\text{ is a generalized permutahedron}\}.

The product
\mu_{S,T}
is defined by

\mu_{S,T}({\mathfrak{p}}\otimes{\mathfrak{q}})={\mathfrak{p}}\times{\mathfrak{q}},

for all permutahedra
{\mathfrak{p}}\in\GP[S]
and
{\mathfrak{q}}\in\GP[T]
.
In particular,
{\sf GP}
is a commutative monoid.
Let
F
be the face of the braid arrangement in
\mathbb{R}^{I}
corresponding to the composition
(S,T)
, and let
v\in\relint(F)
.
Then, for any
{\mathfrak{p}}\in\GP[I]
, the face
{\mathfrak{p}}_{v}
decomposes as a product of generalized permutahedra
{\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\times{\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}
, with
{\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\in\GP[S]
and
{\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}\in\GP[T]
, see
[
1
, Proposition 5.2]
.
The coproduct is defined by

\Delta_{S,T}({\mathfrak{p}})={\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\otimes{\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}.

Aguiar and Ardila also give the following grouping-free and cancellation-free formula for its antipode.
For a generalized permutahedron
{\mathfrak{p}}\in\GP[I]
,

\apode_{I}({\mathfrak{p}})=(-1)^{|I|}\sum_{{\mathfrak{q}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}}(-1)^{\dim({\mathfrak{q}})}{\mathfrak{q}}.

We now introduce the subspecies
{\sf Mc}
of
{\sf GP}
.
The space
{\sf Mc}[I]\subseteq{\sf GP}[I]
is the subspace spanned by elements

(7.1)

{\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}+{\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}-{\mathfrak{p}}-{\mathfrak{q}}\qquad\text{for}\quad{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{q}}\in\GP[I]\text{ such that }{\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}\text{ is convex},

and

(7.2)

{\mathfrak{p}}_{+t}-{\mathfrak{p}}\qquad\text{for}\quad{\mathfrak{p}}\in\GP[I]\text{ and }t\in\mathbb{R}^{I},

where
{\mathfrak{p}}_{+t}
denotes the Minkowski sum
{\mathfrak{p}}+\{t\}
.
The sums and differences in (
7.1
) and (
7.2
) correspond to the vector space structure of
{\sf GP}[I]
, not to Minkowski sum or difference.

Remark 7.1
.

Recall from Section
2.2
that if
{\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}
is a polytope, then
{\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}
and
{\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}
are necessarily generalized permutahedra.
Thus, the elements (
7.1
) are indeed in
{\sf GP}[I]
.

The following result shows that
{\sf Mc}
defines relations compatible with the Hopf monoid structure of
{\sf GP}
.
Ardila and Sanchez
[
8
]
prove a similar result for extended generalized permutahedra by realizing
{\sf Mc}
as the kernel of a Hopf monoid morphism.

Theorem 7.2
.

The subspecies
{\sf Mc}
is an ideal and a coideal of
{\sf GP}
.
That is,

\mu_{S,T}\big({\sf Mc}[S]\otimes{\sf GP}[T]\big)\subseteq{\sf Mc}[I]\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta_{S,T}\big({\sf Mc}[I]\big)\subseteq{\sf Mc}[S]\otimes{\sf GP}[T]+{\sf GP}[S]\otimes{\sf Mc}[T],

for any
I=S\sqcup T
.
Therefore, the quotient species
\widetilde{\Pi}
defined by

\widetilde{\Pi}[I]={\sf GP}[I]/{\sf Mc}[I]

inherits the Hopf monoid structure of
{\sf GP}
.

Proof.

For generators of
{\sf Mc}
of the form (
7.2
), the result follows from the following two observations.
If
{\mathfrak{p}}\in\GP[S]
,
{\mathfrak{r}}\in\GP[T]
and
t\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
, then

{\mathfrak{p}}_{+t}\times{\mathfrak{r}}=({\mathfrak{p}}\times{\mathfrak{r}})_{+(t,0)}.

If
{\mathfrak{p}}\in\GP[I]
and
t\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
, then

\Delta_{S,T}({\mathfrak{p}}_{+t})=({\mathfrak{p}}|_{S})_{+t_{S}}\otimes({\mathfrak{p}}|_{S})_{+t_{T}},

where
t_{S}
and
t_{T}
denote the projections of
t
to
\mathbb{R}^{S}
and
\mathbb{R}^{T}
, respectively.

We will now focus on generators of
{\sf Mc}
of the form (
7.1
).
Fix an arbitrary finite set
I
and a nontrivial decomposition
I=S\sqcup T
.
Let
v\in\mathbb{R}^{I}
be any vector in the interior of the corresponding face of the braid arrangement.

Suppose
{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{q}},{\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\sf GP}[S]
and
{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\sf GP}[T]
.
Then,

({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}})\times{\mathfrak{r}}=({\mathfrak{p}}\times{\mathfrak{r}})\cup({\mathfrak{q}}\times{\mathfrak{r}}),\qquad\qquad({\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}})\times{\mathfrak{r}}=({\mathfrak{p}}\times{\mathfrak{r}})\cap({\mathfrak{q}}\times{\mathfrak{r}}),

and
({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}})\times{\mathfrak{r}}=({\mathfrak{p}}\times{\mathfrak{r}})\cup({\mathfrak{q}}\times{\mathfrak{r}})
is a polytope if and only if
{\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}
is.
It follows that

\mu_{S,T}\big(({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}+{\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}-{\mathfrak{p}}-{\mathfrak{q}})\otimes{\mathfrak{r}}\big)=({\mathfrak{p}}\times{\mathfrak{r}})\cup({\mathfrak{q}}\times{\mathfrak{r}})+({\mathfrak{p}}\times{\mathfrak{r}})\cap({\mathfrak{q}}\times{\mathfrak{r}})-{\mathfrak{p}}\times{\mathfrak{r}}-{\mathfrak{q}}\times{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\sf Mc}[I].

Since
{\sf GP}
is commutative, this proves that
{\sf Mc}
is an ideal.

Now, let
{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{q}},{\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\sf GP}[I]
.
There are two possibilities:

i.

The face
({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}})_{v}
of
{\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}
is completely contained in
{\mathfrak{p}}
or in
{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Without loss of generality, suppose the former.
Then
({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}})_{v}={\mathfrak{p}}_{v}
and, necessarily,
({\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}})_{v}={\mathfrak{q}}_{v}
.
Hence,

\Delta_{S,T}\big({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}+{\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}-{\mathfrak{p}}-{\mathfrak{q}}\big)=\Delta_{S,T}({\mathfrak{p}})+\Delta_{S,T}({\mathfrak{q}})-\Delta_{S,T}({\mathfrak{p}})-\Delta_{S,T}({\mathfrak{q}})=0

ii.

The face
({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}})_{v}
is not contained in
{\mathfrak{p}}
nor in
{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Hence,
({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}})_{v}={\mathfrak{p}}_{v}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}_{v}
and
({\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}})_{v}={\mathfrak{p}}_{v}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}_{v}
.
Expanding the first equality we have

({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}})|_{S}\times({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}})/_{S}=({\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\times{\mathfrak{p}}/_{S})\cup({\mathfrak{q}}|_{S}\times{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S}).

The union of two Cartesian products
A\times B
and
C\times D
is again a Cartesian product if and only if one contains the other or either
A=C
or
B=D
.
By assumption, the is no containment between
{\mathfrak{p}}_{v}
and
{\mathfrak{q}}_{v}
.
We can therefore assume without loss of generality that

(7.3)

{\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}={\mathfrak{q}}|_{S}.

Projecting to
\mathbb{R}^{S}
and
\mathbb{R}^{T}
, we further see that

(7.4)

({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}})|_{S}={\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}|_{S}={\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\qquad\text{and}\qquad({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}})/_{S}={\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S}.

In particular,
{\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S}
is a generalized permutahedron.
On the other hand, expanding
({\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}})_{v}={\mathfrak{p}}_{v}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}_{v}
, we have

({\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}})|_{S}\times({\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}})/_{S}=({\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\times{\mathfrak{p}}/_{S})\cap({\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\times{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S})={\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\times({\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S}).

Comparing factors, we deduce

(7.5)

({\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}})|_{S}={\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\qquad\text{and}\qquad({\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}})/_{S}={\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S}.

Putting together (
7.3
), (
7.4
) and (
7.5
), we conclude

\Delta_{S,T}\big({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}+{\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}-{\mathfrak{p}}-{\mathfrak{q}}\big)=\Delta_{S,T}({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}})+\Delta_{S,T}({\mathfrak{q}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}})-\Delta_{S,T}({\mathfrak{p}})-\Delta_{S,T}({\mathfrak{q}})\\
={\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\otimes({\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S})+{\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\otimes({\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S})-{\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\otimes{\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}-{\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\otimes{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S}\\
={\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}\otimes\big({\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S}+{\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S}-{\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}-{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S}\big)\in{\sf GP}[S]\otimes{\sf Mc}[T].

Thus, in either case we get
\Delta_{S,T}\big({\mathfrak{p}}\cup{\mathfrak{q}}+{\mathfrak{p}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}-{\mathfrak{p}}-{\mathfrak{q}}\big)\in{\sf Mc}[S]\otimes{\sf GP}[T]+{\sf GP}[S]\otimes{\sf Mc}[T]
. That is,
{\sf Mc}
is a coideal of
{\sf GP}
.
∎

Comparing the generators of the (co)ideal
{\sf Mc}
with the relations defining McMullen’s polytope algebra, it is natural to ask if
\widetilde{\Pi}[I]
agrees with
\Pi(\pi_{I})
, where
\pi_{I}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{I}
is the standard permutahedron.
The answer is no.
For instance, in the polytope algebra, the structure of
\mathbb{R}
-vector space is defined so that

\alpha\big(\big[\begin{gathered}\hbox to23.96pt{\vbox to12.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-0.59999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

{}{}}}{
{}{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

where the numbers over the segments denote their length.
However, if
\alpha
is an irrational number, then

\big(\alpha\begin{gathered}\hbox to23.96pt{\vbox to12.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-0.59999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}-\alpha\bullet\big)-\big(\begin{gathered}\hbox to29.65pt{\vbox to10.88pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-0.59999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}-\bullet\big)\notin{\sf Mc}[I].

Nevertheless, the proof of the previous theorem works verbatim to show that the Hopf monoid operations are well-defined in
\Pi(\pi_{I})
.

Theorem 7.3
.

The species
\Pi
defined by
\Pi[I]=\Pi(\pi_{I})
is a Hopf monoid, with product and coproduct defined for any decomposition
I=S\sqcup T
by

\mu_{S,T}([{\mathfrak{q}}_{1}]\otimes[{\mathfrak{q}}_{2}])=[{\mathfrak{q}}_{1}\times{\mathfrak{q}}_{2}]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{S,T}([{\mathfrak{p}}])=[{\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}]\otimes[{\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}]

for all classes of generalized permutahedra
[{\mathfrak{q}}_{1}]\in\Pi[S]
,
[{\mathfrak{q}}_{2}]\in\Pi[T]
, and
[{\mathfrak{p}}]\in\Pi[I]
.
Moreover,
\Pi
is a Hopf monoid quotient of
{\sf GP}
via the morphism
{\mathfrak{p}}\mapsto[{\mathfrak{p}}]
.

It immediately follows from the definitions above that, if
F\in\Sigma[\mathcal{A}_{d}]
is the face corresponding to a composition
(S,T)
of
[d]
, then

[{\mathfrak{p}}]\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}=\mu_{S,T}\circ\Delta_{S,T}([{\mathfrak{p}}])

for all generalized permutahedra
{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Thus, in the case of generalized permutahedra, the module structure of Section
5
is precisely the one induced from the Hopf monoid structure above.

The antipode formula of
{\sf GP}
descends to the quotient
\Pi
, but it is no longer grouping-free in general.
The Euler map (
2.7
) allows us to write the antipode formula of
\Pi
in a very compact form:

\apode_{I}([{\mathfrak{p}}])=(-1)^{|I|}[{\mathfrak{p}}]^{*}.

7.3.
Higher monoidal structures

We have just proved that
\Pi
is a Hopf monoid in the symmetric monoidal category
({\sf Sp},\bm{\cdot})
.
The algebra structure of each space
\Pi[I]
defined by McMullen can also be defined for
{\sf GP}
.
In both cases, this endows the species with the structure of a monoid in the symmetric monoidal category
({\sf Sp},\times)
of species with the
Hadamard product
.
The Hadamard product of two species
{\mathsf{p}}
and
{\mathsf{q}}
is defined by

({\mathsf{p}}\times{\mathsf{q}})[I]={\mathsf{p}}[I]\otimes{\mathsf{q}}[I].

Hence, a monoid in
({\sf Sp},\times)
consists of a species
{\mathsf{p}}
with an algebra structure on each space
{\mathsf{p}}[I]
.
For generalized permutahedra, these structures are compatible in a very special way.

Theorem 7.4
.

The species of generalized permutahedra
{\sf GP}
and its quotient
\Pi
are
(2,1)
-monoids in the 3-monoidal category
({\sf Sp},\bm{\cdot},\times,\bm{\cdot})
.

See
[
3
, Chapter 7]
for the definition of higher monoidal categories and of monoids in such categories.
The notation
(2,1)
indicates that
{\sf GP}
is a monoid with respect to the first two monoidal structures (Cartesian product and Minkowski sum, respectively) and a comonoid with respect to the last (coproduct maps
\Delta_{S,T}
).

Proof.

We only discuss the remaining compatibility axioms:
the compatibility between Cartesian product and Minkowski sum, and the compatibility between Minkowski sum and the coproduct.

The compatibility between Cartesian product and Minkowski sum boils down to the identity

({\mathfrak{p}}_{1}+{\mathfrak{p}}_{2})\times({\mathfrak{q}}_{1}+{\mathfrak{q}}_{2})=({\mathfrak{p}}_{1}\times{\mathfrak{q}}_{1})+({\mathfrak{p}}_{2}\times{\mathfrak{q}}_{2})

for
{\mathfrak{p}}_{1},{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}\in\GP[S]
and
{\mathfrak{q}}_{1},{\mathfrak{q}}_{2}\in\GP[T]
, which one easily verifies for arbitrary sets
{\mathfrak{p}}_{1},{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{S}
and
{\mathfrak{q}}_{1},{\mathfrak{q}}_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{T}
.

On the other hand, the compatibility between Minkowski sum and the coproduct is equivalent to the following identity for generalized permutahedra
{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{q}}\in\GP[I]
:

({\mathfrak{p}}+{\mathfrak{q}})|_{S}\otimes({\mathfrak{p}}+{\mathfrak{q}})/_{S}=({\mathfrak{p}}|_{S}+{\mathfrak{q}}|_{S})\otimes({\mathfrak{p}}/_{S}+{\mathfrak{q}}/_{S}).

This follows by projecting the identity

({\mathfrak{p}}+{\mathfrak{q}})_{v}={\mathfrak{p}}_{v}+{\mathfrak{q}}_{v}

to
\mathbb{R}^{S}
and
\mathbb{R}^{T}
, respectively, where
v
is any vector in the interior of the face of the braid arrangement corresponding to the composition
(S,T)
.
∎

The compatibility between Minkowski sum and the Hopf monoid operations refines the last statement in Theorem
4.3
; which, in the language of this section, states that the maps
\mu_{S,T}\circ\Delta_{S,T}
are compatible with the Minkowski sum operation.

8.
Final remarks and questions

1

Eulerian numbers are defined for any Coxeter group
W
in terms of
W
-descents.
For the Coxeter groups of type A and B, descents and (B-)excedances are equally distributed, so we can interpret the
W
-Eulerian polynomials as the generating functions for (B-)excedances.
However, the joint distributions of
\big(|\supp(\cdot)|,\des(\cdot)\big)
and
\big(|\supp(\cdot)|,\exc(\cdot)\big)
do not agree.
Therefore, Theorems
5.1
and
6.1
cannot immediately be expressed in terms of descents.

Extending the results of this section to other Coxeter groups
W
requires to find the correct notion of
W
-excedance for other types.
Is there a nice analog of the result of Ardila, Benedetti, and Doker, and of Theorem
6.9
in type D?

2

McMullen
[
21
]
also studied valuation relations for the collection of polyhedral cones in
V
.
The
full cone group
of
V
is generated by the classes
[C]
, one for each polyhedral cone
C\subseteq V
.
They satisfy the following relation:

(8.1)

[C_{1}\cup C_{2}]=[C_{1}]+[C_{2}]

whenever
C_{1}\cup C_{2}
is a cone and
C_{1}\cap C_{2}
is a proper face of
C_{1}
and of
C_{2}
.
Note that this is not to say that the class
[C_{1}\cap C_{2}]
is zero.

A cone of an arrangement
\mathcal{A}
is any convex cone obtained as the union of faces of
\mathcal{A}
.
The space of formal linear combinations of cones
\Omega[\mathcal{A}]
is a right
\mathsf{\Sigma}[\mathcal{A}]
module under the following operation.
If
C
is a cone of
\mathcal{A}
and
F\in\Sigma[\mathcal{A}]
, then

C\cdot\mathtt{H}_{F}=\begin{cases}T_{\tilde{F}}C&\text{if }F\subseteq C,\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where
\tilde{F}\leq C
is the minimum face of
C
containing
F
and
T_{\tilde{F}}C
denotes the tangent cone of
C
at
\tilde{F}
. The relation (
8.1
) is compatible with this action, and defines a quotient module
\overline{\Omega}[\mathcal{A}]
.

Restricting to the case of all the braid arrangements,
\Omega
defines a Hopf monoid in species. The product is defined by means of the Cartesian product. Let
C
be a cone of the braid arrangement in
\mathbb{R}^{I}
and
F
the face corresponding to the composition
(S,T)\vDash I
. If
F\subseteq C
, the tangent cone
T_{\tilde{F}}C
decomposes as a product
C|_{S}\times C/_{S}
of cones in
\mathbb{R}^{S}
and
\mathbb{R}^{T}
. The coproduct of
\Omega
is defined as follows:

\Delta_{S,T}(C)=\begin{cases}C|_{S}\otimes C/_{S}&\text{if }F\subseteq C\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

With these operations,
\Omega
is isomorphic to the Hopf monoid of preposets
{\bf Q}
considered in
[
1
]
.
Relation (
8.1
) defines a Hopf monoid quotient
\overline{\Omega}
.
Under a suitable change of basis,
\overline{\Omega}
is isomorphic to the dual Hopf monoid of faces
{\bf\Sigma}^{*}
defined in
[
3
, Chapter 12]
.

3

There is a Hopf monoid morphism
{\sf GP}\rightarrow\Omega
, whose components
{\sf GP}[I]\rightarrow\Omega[I]
are defined as follows:

(8.2)

{\mathfrak{p}}\longmapsto\sum_{{\mathfrak{q}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}}N({\mathfrak{q}},{\mathfrak{p}})=\sum_{C\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}C.

Moreover, this is a morphism of
(2,1)
-monoids in the 3-monoidal category
({\sf Sp},\bm{\cdot},\times,\bm{\cdot})
, where the monoidal structure of
\Omega
under the Hadamard product is given by

C_{1}\cdot C_{2}=\begin{cases}C_{1}\cap C_{2}&\text{ if }\relint(C_{1})\cap\relint(C_{2})\neq\emptyset,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

That this map defines a morphism of monoids under the Hadamard product is equivalent to the following fact: the normal fan of
{\mathfrak{p}}+{\mathfrak{q}}
is the common refinement of
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\Sigma_{q}
.

The map (
8.2
) does
not
induce a well defined morphism
\Pi\rightarrow\overline{\Omega}
.
In
[
21
, Theorem 5]
, McMullen shows that

{\mathfrak{p}}\longmapsto\sum_{{\mathfrak{q}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}}\vol({\mathfrak{q}})N({\mathfrak{q}},{\mathfrak{p}})

induces an injective map
\Pi[I]\rightarrow\overline{\Omega}[I]
, where
\vol({\mathfrak{q}})
is the
normalized
volume of
{\mathfrak{q}}
in the affine space spanned by
{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Moreover, one can verify that the induced morphism
\Pi\rightarrow\overline{\Omega}
is a morphism of Hopf monoids.
Is it possible to endow
\overline{\Omega}
with the structure of a
(2,1)
-monoid so that the morphism above is a morphism of
(2,1)
-monoids?

Such a structure on
\bar{\Omega}[I]
would contain a subalgebra isomorphic to the Möbius algebra
B^{*}(M)
introduced by Huh and Wang in
[
19
, Definition 5]
, where
M
is the matroid associated with the braid arrangement
\mathcal{A}_{I}
in
\mathbb{R}^{I}
.
